On 26 April 1986, reactor 4 of the [Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant](/wiki/Chernobyl_Nuclear_Power_Plant "Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant"), located near [Pripyat, Ukraine](/wiki/Pripyat "Pripyat"), exploded. With dozens of direct casualties and thousands of health complications stemming from the disaster, it is one of only two [nuclear accidents](/wiki/Nuclear_and_radiation_accidents_and_incidents "Nuclear and radiation accidents and incidents") rated at the maximum severity on the [International Nuclear Event Scale](/wiki/International_Nuclear_and_Radiological_Event_Scale "International Nuclear and Radiological Event Scale"), the other being the 2011 [Fukushima nuclear accident](/wiki/Fukushima_nuclear_accident "Fukushima nuclear accident"), which happened in [Japan](/wiki/Japan "Japan").[](#cite_note-1) The response involved more than 500,000 [personnel](/wiki/Chernobyl_liquidators "Chernobyl liquidators") and cost an estimated 18 billion [rubles](/wiki/Soviet_ruble "Soviet ruble") (about $85 billion in 2026).[](#cite_note-OECD02-Ch2-2) It remains the worst nuclear disaster[](#cite_note-3)[](#cite_note-4) and the [most expensive disaster in history](/wiki/List_of_disasters_by_cost "List of disasters by cost"), with an estimated cost of US$700 billion.[](#cite_note-5) The disaster occurred during a test to simulate the cooling of the [reactor](/wiki/Nuclear_reactor "Nuclear reactor") during a serious accident in [blackout](/wiki/Power_outage "Power outage") conditions. The operators carried out the test following an accidental drop in reactor power. Upon shutting down the reactor in such conditions, pervasive design flaws led to a power surge. The reactor components ruptured and lost [coolant](/wiki/Coolant "Coolant"), and the resulting steam explosions and [meltdown](/wiki/Nuclear_meltdown "Nuclear meltdown") destroyed the reactor building. This was followed by a reactor core fire that spread [radioactive contaminants](/wiki/Radioactive_contamination "Radioactive contamination") across the Soviet Union and [Europe](/wiki/Europe "Europe").[](#cite_note-6) The Soviet government established a 10 km [exclusion zone](/wiki/Chernobyl_exclusion_zone "Chernobyl exclusion zone") 36 hours after the accident, initially evacuating around 49,000 people. This was later expanded to 30 km, resulting in the evacuation of approximately 68,000 more people.[](#cite_note-Nuclear_Disasters_pp_55-7) The government did not publicly acknowledge the disaster until two days after the explosion, when elevated radiation levels were detected in [Sweden](/wiki/Sweden "Sweden").[](#cite_note-Sveriges-8)

Following the explosion, which killed two engineers and severely burned two others, an emergency operation began to put out the fires and stabilize the reactor. Of the 237 workers hospitalized, 134 showed symptoms of [acute radiation syndrome](/wiki/Acute_radiation_syndrome "Acute radiation syndrome") (ARS); 28 of them died within three months. Over the next decade, 14 more workers (nine of whom had ARS) [died](/wiki/Deaths_due_to_the_Chernobyl_disaster "Deaths due to the Chernobyl disaster") of various causes mostly unrelated to radiation exposure.[](#cite_note-:5-9) It is the only instance in commercial nuclear power history where radiation-related fatalities occurred.[](#cite_note-10)[](#cite_note-11) As of 2005, 6000 cases of childhood [thyroid cancer](/wiki/Thyroid_cancer "Thyroid cancer") occurred within the affected populations (15 of them fatal), "a large fraction" being attributed to the accident.[](#cite_note-WHO2012-12) Long-term death estimates range from up to 4,000 in Ukraine, Belarus, and Russia (per the [United Nations](/wiki/United_Nations "United Nations")) to 16,000 in total across Europe.[](#cite_note-World_Health_Organization_report_ex-13)

Pripyat was abandoned and replaced by the purpose-built city of [Slavutych](/wiki/Slavutych "Slavutych"). The [Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant sarcophagus](/wiki/Chernobyl_Nuclear_Power_Plant_sarcophagus "Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant sarcophagus"), completed in December 1986, reduced the spread of [radioactive contamination](/wiki/Radioactive_contamination "Radioactive contamination") and provided [radiological protection](/wiki/Radiation_protection "Radiation protection") for the crews of the undamaged reactors. In 2016–2018, the [Chernobyl New Safe Confinement](/wiki/Chernobyl_New_Safe_Confinement "Chernobyl New Safe Confinement") was constructed around the old sarcophagus to enable the removal of the reactor debris, and clean-up is scheduled for completion by 2065.[](#cite_note-14)

## Accident sequence

### Background

#### Reactor cooling after shutdown

[](/wiki/File:Decay_heat_illustration2.PNG)Reactor [decay heat](/wiki/Decay_heat "Decay heat") shown as % of thermal power from time of sustained fission shutdown using two different correlations. Due to decay heat, solid fuel power reactors need high flows of coolant after a fission shutdown for a considerable time to prevent [fuel cladding damage](/wiki/Behavior_of_nuclear_fuel_during_a_reactor_accident "Behavior of nuclear fuel during a reactor accident"), or in the worst case, a full [core meltdown](/wiki/Core_melt_accident "Core melt accident").

In nuclear-reactor operation, most heat is generated by [nuclear fission](/wiki/Nuclear_fission "Nuclear fission"), but over 6% comes from [radioactive decay](/wiki/Radioactive_decay "Radioactive decay"), which continues after the reactor shuts down. Continued coolant circulation is essential to prevent core overheating or a [core meltdown](/wiki/Core_meltdown "Core meltdown").[](#cite_note-15) [RBMK](/wiki/RBMK "RBMK") reactors, like those at Chernobyl, use water as a coolant, circulated by electrically driven pumps.[](#cite_note-16)[](#cite_note-17) Reactor no.‍4 had 1,661 individual fuel channels, requiring over 45 million litres of coolant per hour for the entire reactor.[](#cite_note-18)[_[failed verification](/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_]

In case of a total power loss, each of Chernobyl's reactors had three backup [diesel generators](/wiki/Diesel_generator "Diesel generator"), but they took 60–75 seconds to reach full load and generate the 5.5 MW needed to run one main pump.[](#cite_note-MedvedevZ-19): 15 Special counterweights on each pump provided coolant via [inertia](/wiki/Inertia "Inertia") to bridge the gap to generator startup.[](#cite_note-20)[](#cite_note-21) However, a potential safety risk existed in the event that a station blackout occurred simultaneously with the rupture of a coolant pipe. In this scenario the [emergency core cooling system](/wiki/Emergency_core_cooling_system "Emergency core cooling system") (ECCS) is needed to pump additional water into the core.[](#cite_note-insag7-22)

It had been theorized that the [rotational momentum](/wiki/Angular_momentum "Angular momentum") of the reactor's [steam turbine](/wiki/Steam_turbine "Steam turbine") could be used to generate the required electrical power to operate the ECCS via the [feedwater pumps](/wiki/Feedwater_heater "Feedwater heater"). The turbine's speed would run down as energy was taken from it, but that there might have been sufficient energy to provide electrical power to run the coolant pumps for 45 seconds.[](#cite_note-MedvedevZ-19): 16 This would not quite bridge the gap between an external power failure and the full availability of the emergency generators, but would alleviate the situation.[](#cite_note-NV_Karpan:_312–13-23)

#### Safety test

The turbine run-down energy capability still needed to be confirmed experimentally, and previous tests had ended unsuccessfully. An initial test carried out in 1982 indicated that the [excitation](/wiki/Excitation_\(magnetic\) "Excitation \(magnetic\)") voltage of the turbine-generator was insufficient. The electrical system was modified and the test was repeated in 1984, but again proved unsuccessful. In 1985, the test was conducted a third time, but also yielded no results due to a problem with the recording equipment. The test procedure was to be run again in 1986 and was scheduled to take place during a controlled power-down of reactor no.‍4, which was preparatory to a planned maintenance outage.[](#cite_note-NV_Karpan:_312–13-23)[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 51

A test procedure had been written, but the authors were not aware of the unusual RBMK-1000 reactor behaviour under the planned operating conditions.[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 52 It was regarded as purely an electrical test of the generator, even though it involved critical unit systems. According to the existing regulations, such a test did not require approval by either the chief design authority for the reactor (NIKIET) or the nuclear safety regulator.[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 51–52 The test program called for disabling the [emergency core cooling system](/wiki/Emergency_core_cooling_system "Emergency core cooling system"), a passive/active system of core cooling intended to provide water to the core in a [loss-of-coolant accident](/wiki/Loss-of-coolant_accident "Loss-of-coolant accident"). Approval from the site chief engineer had been obtained according to regulations.[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 18

The test procedure was intended to run as follows:[](#cite_note-:12-24)

  1. The reactor thermal power was to be reduced to between 700 MW and 1,000 MW (to allow for adequate cooling, as the turbine would be spun at operating speed while disconnected from the power grid).
  2. The steam-turbine generator was to be run at normal operating speed.
  3. Four out of eight main circulating pumps were to be supplied with off-site power, while the other four would be powered by the turbine.
  4. When the correct conditions were achieved, the steam supply to the turbine generator would be closed, which would trigger an automatic reactor shutdown in ordinary conditions.
  5. The voltage provided by the coasting turbine would be measured, along with the voltage and revolutions per minute (RPMs) of the four main circulating pumps being powered by the turbine.
  6. When the emergency generators supplied full electrical power, the turbine generator would be allowed to continue free-wheeling down.

#### Test delay and shift change

[](/wiki/File:RBMK_en.svg)Process flow diagram of the reactor [](/wiki/File:Gen_II_nuclear_reactor_vessels_sizes.svg)Size comparison of [Generation II reactor](/wiki/Generation_II_reactor "Generation II reactor") vessels, a design classification of commercial reactors built until the end of the 1990s. The RBMK reactor is depicted as a gray rectangle.

The test was to be conducted during the day-shift of 25 April 1986 as part of a scheduled reactor shutdown. The day shift had been instructed in advance on the reactor operating conditions to run the test, and a special team of [electrical engineers](/wiki/Electrical_engineer "Electrical engineer") was present to conduct the electrical test once the correct conditions were reached.[](#cite_note-25) As planned, a gradual reduction in the output of the power unit began at 01:06 on 25 April, and the power level had reached 1600 MW of its nominal 3,200 MW thermal level by the beginning of the day shift.[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 53

The day shift was scheduled to perform the test at 14:15.[](#cite_note-Karpan44-26): 3 Preparations for the test were carried out, including the disabling of the [emergency core cooling system](/wiki/Emergency_core_cooling_system "Emergency core cooling system").[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 53 Meanwhile, another regional power station unexpectedly went offline. At 14:00,[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 53 the [Kiev](/wiki/Kyiv "Kyiv") electrical grid controller requested that the further reduction of Chernobyl's output be postponed, as power was needed to satisfy peak evening demand. 

Soon, the day shift was replaced by the evening shift.[](#cite_note-Karpan44-26): 3 Despite the delay, the emergency core cooling system was left disabled. This system had to be disconnected via a manual isolating slide valve,[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 51 which in practice meant that two or three people spent the whole shift manually turning sailboat-helm–sized valve wheels.[](#cite_note-Karpan44-26): 4 The system had no influence on the disaster, but allowing the reactor to run for 11 hours outside of the test without emergency protection was indicative of a general lack of [safety culture](/wiki/Safety_culture "Safety culture").[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 10, 18

At 23:04, the Kiev grid controller allowed the reactor shutdown to resume. The day shift had long since departed, the evening shift was also preparing to leave, and the night shift would not take over until midnight, well into the job. According to plan, the test should have been finished during the day shift, and the night shift would only have had to maintain decay heat cooling systems in an otherwise shut-down plant.[](#cite_note-MedvedevZ-19): 36–38

The night shift had very limited time to prepare for and carry out the experiment. [Anatoly Dyatlov](/wiki/Anatoly_Dyatlov "Anatoly Dyatlov"), deputy chief-engineer of the [Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant](/wiki/Chernobyl_Nuclear_Power_Plant "Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant") (ChNPP), was present to direct the test. He was one of the test's chief authors and he was the highest-ranking individual present. Unit Shift Supervisor [Aleksandr Akimov](/wiki/Aleksandr_Akimov "Aleksandr Akimov") was in charge of the Unit 4 night shift, and [Leonid Toptunov](/wiki/Leonid_Toptunov "Leonid Toptunov") was the Senior Reactor Control Engineer responsible for the reactor's operational regimen, including the movement of the [control rods](/wiki/Control_rod "Control rod"). 25-year-old Toptunov had worked independently as a senior engineer for approximately three months.[](#cite_note-MedvedevZ-19): 36–38

#### Unexpected drop of the reactor power

The test plan called for a gradual decrease in reactor power to a thermal level of 700–1000 MW,[](#cite_note-27) and an output of 720 MW was reached at 00:05 on 26 April.[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 53 However, due to the reactor's production of a fission byproduct, [xenon-135](/wiki/Xenon-135 "Xenon-135"), which is a reaction-inhibiting [neutron absorber](/wiki/Neutron_absorber "Neutron absorber"), power continued to decrease in the absence of further operator action, a process known as [reactor poisoning](/wiki/Reactor_poisoning "Reactor poisoning"). In steady-state operation, this is avoided because xenon-135 is "burned off" as quickly as it is created, becoming the highly stable [xenon-136](/wiki/Xenon-136 "Xenon-136"). With reactor power reduced, large quantities of previously produced [iodine-135](/wiki/Iodine-135 "Iodine-135") were decaying into the neutron-absorbing xenon-135 faster than the reduced [neutron flux](/wiki/Neutron_flux "Neutron flux") could "burn it off".[](#cite_note-nf-28) Xenon poisoning in this context made reactor control more difficult, but was a predictable phenomenon during such a power reduction. 

When the reactor power had decreased to approximately 500 MW, the reactor power control was switched from local automatic regulator to the automatic regulators, to manually maintain the required power level.[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 11 AR-1 then activated, removing all four of AR-1's control rods automatically, but AR-2 failed to activate due to an imbalance in its ionization chambers. In response, Toptunov reduced power to stabilize the automatic regulators' ionization sensors. The result was a sudden power drop to an unintended near-[shutdown](/wiki/Shutdown_\(nuclear_reactor\) "Shutdown \(nuclear reactor\)") state, with a power output of 30 MW thermal or less. The exact circumstances that caused the power drop are unknown. Most reports attribute the power drop to Toptunov's error, but Dyatlov reported that it was due to a fault in the AR-2 system.[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 11

The reactor was now producing only 5% of the minimum initial power level prescribed for the test.[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 73 This low reactivity inhibited the burn-off of xenon-135[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 6 within the reactor core and hindered the rise of reactor power. To increase power, control-room personnel removed numerous control rods from the reactor.[](#cite_note-29) Several minutes elapsed before the reactor was restored to 160 MW at 00:39, at which point most control rods were at their upper limits, but the rod configuration was still within its normal limit, calculated as equivalent to having more than 15 rods inserted. Over the next twenty minutes, reactor power would be increased further to 200 MW.[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 73

#### Reactor conditions priming the accident

When an output of 200 MW was reached, preparation for the experiment continued, although power was still much lower than the prescribed 700 MW. As part of the test, two additional main circulating pumps were activated at 01:05. The increased coolant flow lowered the overall core temperature and reduced bubbles (voids) from boiling coolant. Because water [absorbs neutrons](/wiki/Neutron_capture "Neutron capture") better than steam, neutron flux and power accordingly decreased. The operators responded by removing more manual control rods to maintain power,[](#cite_note-OECD02-Ch1-30)[](#cite_note-31) resulting in the inserted control rod equivalent value falling below its required level of 15. This was not apparent to the operators, because there were no instruments capable of calculating this value in real time.[_(/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]

These actions left the reactor in an extremely unstable state. Nearly all of the 211 control rods had been withdrawn, and excessive coolant flow prevented the water from adequately cooling between cycles, causing it to re-enter the core at close to boiling point. Unlike other [light-water reactor](/wiki/Light-water_reactor "Light-water reactor") designs, the RBMK design had a positive [void coefficient](/wiki/Void_coefficient "Void coefficient"), meaning that voids formed during boiling had the effect of increasing rather than decreasing core reactivity. More boiling and hence more voids tended to intensify the nuclear chain reaction, which caused yet more boiling. Unknown to the operators, this [positive feedback loop](/wiki/Positive_feedback "Positive feedback") had little to restrain it, and the reactor became highly sensitive to void formation,[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 3, 14 and at risk of an uncontrolled power surge. 

### Accident

#### Test execution

[](/wiki/File:RBMK_Reaktor_ChNPP-4.svg)Plan view of reactor no.‍4 core. The number on each control rod indicates the insertion depth in centimeters one minute prior to the disaster.   
neutron detectors (12)  
control rods (167)  
short control rods from below reactor (32)  
automatic control rods (12)  
pressure tubes with fuel rods (1661)

At 01:23:04, the test began.[](#cite_note-:2-32) Four of the eight main circulating pumps (MCP) were to be powered by voltage from the coasting turbine, while the remaining four pumps received electrical power from the grid as usual. The steam to the turbines was shut off, beginning a run-down of the turbine generator. The diesel generators started and sequentially picked up loads; the generators were to have completely picked up the MCPs' power needs by 01:23:43. As the [momentum](/wiki/Momentum "Momentum") of the turbine generator decreased, so did the power it produced for the pumps. The water flow rate decreased, leading to increased formation of steam voids in the coolant flowing through the fuel pressure tubes.[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 8

#### Reactor shutdown and power excursion

At 01:23:40, as the experiment was wrapping up,[](#cite_note-auto-33) an operator pressed the emergency AZ-5 button to initiate a [scram](/wiki/Scram "Scram") (emergency shutdown) of the reactor,[](#cite_note-34) perhaps in preparation for scheduled maintenance.[](#cite_note-35) The reason for the timing is uncertain, as both Akimov and Toptunov would die shortly after the alarm. The atmosphere in the control room at that point was calm, according to eyewitnesses,[](#cite_note-36)[](#cite_note-37) and there were no active emergency signals at that time. Representatives of the reactor's design team would later argue that the button must have been pressed only after the reactor had begun to self-destruct.[](#cite_note-38): 578 : 85

The AZ-5 button engaged a drive mechanism to fully insert all control rods, including the manual control rods that had been withdrawn earlier. In the [RBMK design](/wiki/RBMK#Control_rods "RBMK"), each control rod had attached to its end a [graphite](/wiki/Graphite "Graphite") neutron moderator extension, the purpose of which was to boost reactor output by displacing neutron-absorbent water when the main part of the control rod was fully withdrawn. When the control rod was at maximum extraction, the graphite extension sat within the water of the core, its neutron-moderating effect operating to increase reactor power.[](#cite_note-insag7-22)

The rods moved at 0.4 m/s, taking 18 to 20 seconds to travel the full height of the [core](/wiki/Nuclear_reactor_core "Nuclear reactor core"), about 7 m (23 ft). As the control rods moved downward into the reactor, their extensions initially displaced neutron-absorbing water with neutron-moderating graphite. It was known that in the RBMK design a scram could therefore initially increase the reaction rate within the core.[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 4 This behaviour had been discovered in 1983, when the insertion of control rods in a similar reactor at [Ignalina Nuclear Power Plant](/wiki/Ignalina_Nuclear_Power_Plant "Ignalina Nuclear Power Plant") had induced a power spike. Countermeasures were, however, not implemented. The IAEA investigative report INSAG-7 later stated, "Apparently, there was a widespread view that the conditions under which the positive scram effect would be important would never occur. However, they did appear in almost every detail in the course of the actions leading to the Chernobyl accident."[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 13

A few seconds into the scram, a power spike occurred,[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 13 and the core overheated, causing some of the [fuel rods](/wiki/Fuel_rod "Fuel rod") to fracture. It has been speculated that this may have blocked the control rods, jamming them at one-third insertion. Within three seconds the reactor's output rose above 530 MW.[](#cite_note-MedvedevZ-19): 31

Instruments did not register the subsequent course of events; they were reconstructed through mathematical simulation. The power spike would have caused an increase in fuel temperature and steam buildup, leading to a rapid increase in [steam pressure](/wiki/Vapor_pressure "Vapor pressure"). This caused the fuel cladding to fail, releasing the fuel elements into the coolant and rupturing the channels in which these elements were located.[](#cite_note-39)

#### Explosions

As the scram continued, the reactor output jumped to around 30,000 MW thermal, 10 times its normal operational output, the last indicated reading on the control panel. According to some estimates, the power spike may have been 10 times higher than that. It was not possible to reconstruct the precise sequence that led to the destruction of the reactor and the power unit building, but a [steam explosion](/wiki/Steam_explosion "Steam explosion") appears to have been the next event. Explosive steam pressure from the damaged fuel channels escaping into the reactor's exterior cooling structure that caused the explosion that destroyed the reactor casing, tearing off and blasting the upper plate called the upper biological shield[](#cite_note-interestingengineering.com-40) (to which the entire reactor assembly was fastened) through the roof of the reactor building. This is believed to be the first explosion that many heard.[](#cite_note-41): 366

This explosion ruptured further fuel channels, as well as severing most of the coolant lines feeding the reactor chamber. As a result, the remaining coolant [flashed](/wiki/Flash_point "Flash point") to steam and escaped the reactor core. The total water loss combined with a high positive void coefficient further increased the reactor's thermal power.[](#cite_note-insag7-22)

A second, more powerful explosion, estimated to have had the power equivalent of 225 tons of TNT,[](#cite_note-DeGeerNuclearJet-42) occurred two or three seconds after the first, dispersing the damaged reactor core and effectively terminating the [nuclear chain reaction](/wiki/Nuclear_chain_reaction "Nuclear chain reaction"). The blast further compromised the reactor containment vessel, ejecting red-hot fragments of graphite moderator and damaged fuel channel material. Observers outside Unit 4 reported burning lumps of material and sparks shooting into the air above the reactor, some of which fell onto the roof of the machine hall and started fires. Approximately 25% of the graphite blocks and overheated material from the fuel channels were expelled.[](#cite_note-MedvedevZ-19): 32 Damage to the building established an airflow through the core, which fanned graphite fires[](#cite_note-MedvedevZ-19): 32 and significantly contributed to the release and spread of radioactive fallout.[](#cite_note-OECD02-Ch1-30)[[a]](#cite_note-45)

After the larger explosion, several employees went outside to get a clearer view. One survivor, [Aleksandr Yuvchenko](/wiki/Individual_involvement_in_the_Chernobyl_disaster#Aleksandr_Yuvchenko "Individual involvement in the Chernobyl disaster"), said that he looked up towards the reactor hall and saw a "very beautiful" laser-like beam of blue light caused by the [ionized-air glow](/wiki/Ionized-air_glow "Ionized-air glow") that appeared to be "flooding up into infinity".[](#cite_note-Meyer-46)[](#cite_note-Bond-47)

### Possible causes of the second explosion

There are several hypotheses about the nature of the second, larger explosion. One view was that the second explosion was caused by the combustion of [hydrogen](/wiki/Hydrogen "Hydrogen"), which had been produced either by the overheated steam-[zirconium](/wiki/Zircaloy "Zircaloy") reaction or by the [reaction of red-hot graphite with steam](/wiki/Syngas "Syngas") that produced hydrogen and [carbon monoxide](/wiki/Carbon_monoxide "Carbon monoxide"). Another hypothesis, by Konstantin Checherov, published in 1998, was that the second explosion was a thermal explosion of the reactor due to the uncontrollable escape of [fast neutrons](/wiki/Fast_neutron "Fast neutron") caused by the complete water loss in the reactor core.[](#cite_note-48)

The force of the second explosion and the ratio of [xenon radioisotopes](/wiki/Isotopes_of_xenon "Isotopes of xenon") released after the accident led Sergei A. Pakhomov and Yuri V. Dubasov to theorize in 2009 that the second explosion could have been an extremely fast nuclear power transient resulting from core material melting in the absence of its water coolant and moderator. Pakhomov and Dubasov argued that there was no delayed supercritical increase in power but a runaway [prompt criticality](/wiki/Prompt_criticality "Prompt criticality"), similar to the explosion of a [fizzled nuclear weapon](/wiki/Fizzle_\(nuclear_test\) "Fizzle \(nuclear test\)").[](#cite_note-Pakhomov2009-49)

Their evidence came from [Cherepovets](/wiki/Cherepovets "Cherepovets"), a city 1000 km northeast of Chernobyl, where physicists from the [V.G. Khlopin Radium Institute](/wiki/V.G._Khlopin_Radium_Institute "V.G. Khlopin Radium Institute") had measured anomalous high levels of [xenon-135](/wiki/Xenon-135 "Xenon-135")—a short half-life isotope—four days after the explosion. This meant that a nuclear event in the reactor may have ejected xenon to higher altitudes in the atmosphere than the later fire did, allowing widespread movement of xenon to remote locations.[](#cite_note-DeGeer-50) This was an alternative to the more accepted explanation of a positive-feedback power excursion where the reactor disassembled itself by a steam explosion.[](#cite_note-insag7-22)[](#cite_note-Pakhomov2009-49) The energy released by the second explosion, which produced the majority of the damage, was estimated by Pakhomov and Dubasov to be at 40 billion [joules](/wiki/Joule "Joule"), the [equivalent](/wiki/TNT_equivalent "TNT equivalent") of about 10 tons of [TNT](/wiki/TNT "TNT").[](#cite_note-Pakhomov2009-49)

The nuclear fizzle hypothesis was examined in 2017 by Lars-Erik De Geer, Christer Persson and Henning Rodhe, who considered such an event to be the more probable cause of the first explosion.[](#cite_note-DeGeerNuclearJet-42): 11 [](#cite_note-51)[](#cite_note-Embury-Dennis-52) Both analyses argue that the nuclear fizzle event, whether producing the second or first explosion, consisted of a [prompt](/wiki/Prompt_neutron "Prompt neutron") chain reaction that was limited to a small portion of the reactor core, since self-disassembly occurs rapidly in fizzle events.[](#cite_note-Pakhomov2009-49)[](#cite_note-DeGeerNuclearJet-42)

### Immediate response

#### Fire containment

[](/wiki/File:Leonid_Telyatnikov_\(1951-2004\)_decorated_in_UK.jpg)Firefighter [Leonid Telyatnikov](/wiki/Leonid_Telyatnikov "Leonid Telyatnikov") being decorated for bravery

Contrary to safety regulations, [bitumen](/wiki/Bitumen "Bitumen"), a combustible material, had been used in the construction of the roof of the reactor building and the turbine hall. Ejected material ignited at least five fires on the roof of adjacent reactor no. 3, which was still operating. It was imperative to put out those fires and protect the cooling systems of reactor no. 3.[](#cite_note-MedvedevZ-19): 42 Inside reactor no. 3, the chief of the night shift, Yuri Bagdasarov, wanted to shut down the reactor immediately, but chief engineer Nikolai Fomin refused. The operators were given [respirators](/wiki/Respirator "Respirator") and [potassium iodide](/wiki/Potassium_iodide "Potassium iodide") tablets and told to continue working. At 05:00, Bagdasarov made his own decision to shut down the reactor,[](#cite_note-MedvedevZ-19): 44 which was confirmed in writing by Dyatlov and Station Shift Supervisor Rogozhkin. 

Shortly after the accident, firefighters arrived.[](#cite_note-:2-32) First on the scene was a Chernobyl Power Station firefighter brigade under the command of Lieutenant [Volodymyr Pravyk](/wiki/Volodymyr_Pravyk "Volodymyr Pravyk"), who was ultimately to die two weeks later of [acute radiation sickness](/wiki/Radiation_poisoning "Radiation poisoning"). They were not told how dangerously radioactive the smoke and the debris were, and may not even have known that the accident was anything more than a regular electrical fire: "We didn't know it was the reactor. No one had told us."[](#cite_note-53) Grigorii Khmel, the driver of one of the fire engines, described what happened: 

> We arrived there at 10 or 15 minutes to two in the morning ... We saw graphite scattered about. Misha asked: "Is that graphite?" I kicked it away. But one of the fighters on the other truck picked it up. "It's hot," he said. The pieces of graphite were of different sizes, some big, some small enough to pick them up [...] We didn't know much about radiation. Even those who worked there had no idea. There was no water left in the trucks. Misha filled a [cistern](/wiki/Cistern "Cistern") and we aimed the water at the top. Then those boys who died went up to the roof—Vashchik, Kolya and others, and Volodya Pravik ... They went up the ladder ... and I never saw them again.[](#cite_note-54)

[](/wiki/File:Ejected_graphite_from_Chernobyl_core.jpg)Image showing a [graphite moderator](/wiki/Neutron_moderator "Neutron moderator") block ejected from the core

Anatoli Zakharov, a fireman stationed in Chernobyl, offered a different description in 2008: "I remember joking to the others, 'There must be an incredible amount of radiation here. We'll be lucky if we're all still alive in the morning.'"[](#cite_note-nuclruss-55) He also stated, "Of course we knew! If we'd followed regulations, we would never have gone near the reactor. But it was a moral obligation—our duty. We were like [kamikaze](/wiki/Kamikaze "Kamikaze")."[](#cite_note-nuclruss-55)

The immediate priority was to extinguish fires on the roof of the station and the area around the building containing reactor no. 4 to protect no. 3. The fires were extinguished by 5:00, but many firefighters received high doses of radiation. The fire inside reactor no. 4 continued to burn until 10 May 1986; it is possible that well over half of the graphite ultimately burned out.[](#cite_note-MedvedevZ-19): 73

In an attempt to seal off radiation, more than 5,000 tonnes of [sand](/wiki/Sand "Sand"), [lead](/wiki/Lead "Lead"), [clay](/wiki/Clay "Clay"), and [neutron-absorbing](/wiki/Neutron_capture "Neutron capture") [boron](/wiki/Boron "Boron") were dropped from helicopters onto the burning reactor, although it was later found that virtually none of those materials had reached the core.[](#cite_note-BBCContaining-56) Around 600 Soviet pilots risked dangerous levels of radiation to fly the thousands of flights needed.[](#cite_note-57)

One firefighter described his experience of the radiation as "tasting like metal", and feeling a sensation similar to [pins and needles](/wiki/Paresthesia "Paresthesia") all over his face. This is consistent with the description given by [Louis Slotin](/wiki/Louis_Slotin "Louis Slotin"), a nuclear physicist of the [Manhattan Project](/wiki/Manhattan_Project "Manhattan Project") who died days after a fatal radiation overdose from [a criticality accident](/wiki/Demon_core#Second_incident "Demon core").[](#cite_note-zeilig22-58)

#### Radiation levels

The explosion and fire threw hot particles of the [nuclear fuel](/wiki/Nuclear_fuel "Nuclear fuel") and more dangerous [fission products](/wiki/Fission_product "Fission product") into the air. The [ionizing radiation](/wiki/Ionizing_radiation "Ionizing radiation") levels in the worst-hit areas of the reactor building have been estimated to be 5.6 [roentgens](/wiki/Roentgen_\(unit\) "Roentgen \(unit\)") per second (R/s), equivalent to more than 20,000 roentgens per hour. A lethal dose is around 500 roentgens (~4.4 [Gray (Gy)](/wiki/Gray_\(unit\) "Gray \(unit\)") in modern radiation units) over five hours. In some areas, unprotected workers received fatal doses in less than a minute. A [dosimeter](/wiki/Dosimeter "Dosimeter") capable of measuring up to 1,000 R/s was buried in the rubble of a collapsed part of the building, and another one failed when turned on. Most remaining dosimeters had limits of 0.001 R/s and therefore read "off scale". The reactor crew could ascertain only that the radiation levels were somewhere above 0.001 R/s (3.6 R/h), while the true levels were vastly higher in some areas.[](#cite_note-MedvedevZ-19): 42–50

Because of inaccurate low readings, reactor crew chief Aleksandr Akimov assumed that the reactor was intact. The evidence of pieces of graphite and reactor fuel lying around the building was ignored, and the readings of another dosimeter brought in by 04:30 were dismissed under the assumption that the new dosimeter must have been defective.[](#cite_note-MedvedevZ-19): 42–50 Akimov stayed in the reactor building until morning, sending members of his crew to try to pump water into the reactor.[_(/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] None of them wore any protective gear. Most, including Akimov, died from radiation exposure within three weeks.[](#cite_note-MedvedevG-59)[](#cite_note-mould-60)

### Accident investigation

Main article: [Investigations into the Chernobyl disaster](/wiki/Investigations_into_the_Chernobyl_disaster "Investigations into the Chernobyl disaster")

The [IAEA](/wiki/IAEA "IAEA") had created the [International Nuclear Safety Advisory Group](/wiki/International_Nuclear_Safety_Group "International Nuclear Safety Group") (INSAG) in 1985.[](#cite_note-61) INSAG produced two significant reports on Chernobyl: INSAG-1 in 1986, and a revised report, INSAG-7, in 1992. According to INSAG-1, the main cause of the accident was the operators' actions, but according to INSAG-7, the main cause was the reactor's design.[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 24 [](#cite_note-62) Both reports identified an inadequate "safety culture" at all managerial and operational levels as a major underlying factor.[](#cite_note-insag7-22): 21, 24

## Crisis management

### Evacuation

[](/wiki/File:View_of_Chernobyl_taken_from_Pripyat_zoomed.JPG)[Pripyat](/wiki/Pripyat "Pripyat") with the [Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant](/wiki/Chernobyl_Nuclear_Power_Plant "Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant") in the distance

The nearby city of Pripyat was not immediately evacuated, and the townspeople were not alerted during the night to what had just happened. Within a few hours, dozens of people fell ill. Later, they reported severe headaches and metallic tastes in their mouths, along with uncontrollable fits of coughing and vomiting.[](#cite_note-TimeDisaster-63) As the plant was run by authorities in Moscow, the government of Ukraine did not receive prompt information on the accident.[](#cite_note-shevchenko-64)

[Valentyna Shevchenko](/wiki/Valentyna_Shevchenko_\(politician\) "Valentyna Shevchenko \(politician\)"), then Chairwoman of the Presidium of [Verkhovna Rada](/wiki/Verkhovna_Rada "Verkhovna Rada") of the Ukrainian SSR, said that Ukraine's acting Minister of Internal Affairs [Vasyl Durdynets](/wiki/Vasyl_Durdynets "Vasyl Durdynets") phoned her at work at 09:00 to report current affairs; only at the end of the conversation did he mention that there had been a fire at the Chernobyl nuclear power plant, adding that it was extinguished and everything was fine. When Shevchenko asked "How are the people?", he replied that there was nothing to be concerned about: "Some are celebrating a wedding, others are gardening, and others are fishing in the [Pripyat River](/wiki/Pripyat_River "Pripyat River")".[](#cite_note-shevchenko-64)

Shevchenko then spoke by telephone to [Volodymyr Shcherbytsky](/wiki/Volodymyr_Shcherbytsky "Volodymyr Shcherbytsky"), [General Secretary of the Communist Party of Ukraine](/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Communist_Party_of_Ukraine "General Secretary of the Communist Party of Ukraine") and _de facto_ head of state, who said he anticipated a delegation of the state commission headed by [Boris Shcherbina](/wiki/Boris_Shcherbina "Boris Shcherbina"), the deputy chairman of the [Council of Ministers of the USSR](/wiki/Government_of_the_Soviet_Union "Government of the Soviet Union").[](#cite_note-shevchenko-64)

[](/wiki/File:Chernobyl_BW_2019_G28.jpg)Ruins of abandoned house in Chernobyl, 2019

A commission was established later in the day to investigate the accident. It was headed by [Valery Legasov](/wiki/Valery_Legasov "Valery Legasov"), First Deputy Director of the Kurchatov Institute of Atomic Energy, and included leading nuclear specialist [Evgeny Velikhov](/wiki/Evgeny_Velikhov "Evgeny Velikhov"), hydro-meteorologist [Yuri Izrael](/wiki/Yuri_Izrael "Yuri Izrael"), radiologist Leonid Ilyin, and others. They flew to [Boryspil International Airport](/wiki/Boryspil_International_Airport "Boryspil International Airport") and arrived at the power plant in the evening of 26 April.[](#cite_note-shevchenko-64) By that time two people had already died and 52 were hospitalized. The delegation soon had ample evidence that the reactor was destroyed and that extremely high levels of radiation had caused a number of cases of radiation exposure. In the early daylight hours of 27 April, they ordered the evacuation of Pripyat.[](#cite_note-shevchenko-64)

[Pripyat evacuation broadcast](/wiki/File:Pripyat_1986.ogg "File:Pripyat 1986.ogg")

Russian language announcement

* * *

_Problems playing this file? See[media help](/wiki/Help:Media "Help:Media")._

A translated excerpt of the evacuation announcement follows:[](#cite_note-pripyat_evacuation_announcement-65)

> For the attention of the residents of Pripyat! The City Council informs you that due to the accident at Chernobyl Power Station in the city of Pripyat the radioactive conditions in the vicinity are deteriorating. The Communist Party, its officials and the armed forces are taking necessary steps to combat this. Nevertheless, with the view to keep people as safe and healthy as possible, the children being top priority, we need to temporarily evacuate the citizens in the nearest towns of Kiev region. For these reasons, starting from 27 April 1986, 14:00 each apartment block will be able to have a bus at its disposal, supervised by the police and the city officials. It is highly advisable to take your documents, some vital personal belongings and a certain amount of food, just in case, with you. The senior executives of public and industrial facilities of the city has decided on the list of employees needed to stay in Pripyat to maintain these facilities in a good working order. All the houses will be guarded by the police during the evacuation period. Comrades, leaving your residences temporarily please make sure you have turned off the lights, electrical equipment and water and shut the windows. Please keep calm and orderly in the process of this short-term evacuation.[](#cite_note-pripyat_evacuation_announcement-65)

[](/wiki/File:P9060463_\(11383823203\).jpg)Abandoned objects in the evacuation zone

To expedite the evacuation, residents were told to bring only what was necessary, and that they would remain evacuated for approximately three days. As a result, most personal belongings were left behind, and residents were only allowed to recover certain items after months had passed. By 15:00, 53,000 people were evacuated to the [Kiev region](/wiki/Kyiv_Oblast "Kyiv Oblast").[](#cite_note-shevchenko-64) The next day, talks began for evacuating people from the 10 km zone.[](#cite_note-shevchenko-64) Ten days after the accident, the evacuation area was expanded to 30 km.[](#cite_note-MarplesSocialImpact-66): 115, 120–121 The [Chernobyl exclusion zone](/wiki/Chernobyl_exclusion_zone "Chernobyl exclusion zone") has remained ever since, although its shape has changed and its size has expanded. 

The surveying and detection of isolated fallout hotspots outside this zone over the following year eventually resulted in 135,000 long-term evacuees in total.[](#cite_note-Nuclear_Disasters_pp_55-7) The years between 1986 and 2000 saw the near tripling in the total number of permanently resettled persons from the most severely contaminated areas to approximately 350,000.[](#cite_note-67)[](#cite_note-68) A new city of [Slavutych](/wiki/Slavutych "Slavutych") has been built across the [Dnieper](/wiki/Dnieper "Dnieper") marshes to house Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant employees instead of Pripyat, with a direct rail connection to the Chernobyl NPP.[](#cite_note-69)

### Official announcement

[](/wiki/File:SPOT-1-1986-05-01-Tchernobyl-PAN.jpg)Picture taken by French satellite [SPOT-1](/wiki/SPOT_\(satellite\) "SPOT \(satellite\)") on 1 May 1986

Evacuation began one and a half days before the accident was publicly acknowledged by the Soviet Union. On the morning of 28 April, radiation levels set off alarms at the [Forsmark Nuclear Power Plant](/wiki/Forsmark_Nuclear_Power_Plant "Forsmark Nuclear Power Plant") in [Sweden](/wiki/Sweden "Sweden"),[](#cite_note-70)[](#cite_note-Sveriges-8) over 1,000 km from the Chernobyl Plant. Workers at Forsmark reported the case to the [Swedish Radiation Safety Authority](/wiki/Swedish_Radiation_Safety_Authority "Swedish Radiation Safety Authority"), which determined that the radiation had originated elsewhere. That day, the Swedish government contacted the Soviet government to inquire about whether there had been a nuclear accident in the Soviet Union. The Soviet authorities initially denied it. It was only after the Swedish government suggested they were about to file an official alert with the [International Atomic Energy Agency](/wiki/International_Atomic_Energy_Agency "International Atomic Energy Agency") that the Soviet government admitted that an accident had taken place at Chernobyl.[](#cite_note-Sveriges-8)[](#cite_note-schmemann19860429-71)

At first, the authorities conceded only that a minor accident had occurred, but once they began evacuating more than 100,000 people, the scale of the situation became widely known.[](#cite_note-72) At 21:02 the evening of 28 April, a 20-second announcement was read in the TV news programme _[Vremya](/wiki/Vremya "Vremya")_ : "There has been an accident at the Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant. One of the nuclear reactors was damaged. The effects of the accident are being remedied. Assistance has been provided for any affected people. An investigative commission has been set up."[](#cite_note-GalleryTimeline-73)[](#cite_note-vremya-74)

This was the first time the Soviet Union had officially announced a nuclear accident. The [Telegraph Agency of the Soviet Union](/wiki/Telegraph_Agency_of_the_Soviet_Union "Telegraph Agency of the Soviet Union") (TASS) then discussed the [Three Mile Island accident](/wiki/Three_Mile_Island_accident "Three Mile Island accident") and other American nuclear accidents, which [Serge Schmemann](/wiki/Serge_Schmemann "Serge Schmemann") of _The New York Times_ wrote was an example of the common Soviet tactic of [whataboutism](/wiki/Whataboutism "Whataboutism"). The mention of a commission also indicated to observers the seriousness of the incident,[](#cite_note-schmemann19860429-71) and subsequent state radio broadcasts were replaced with classical music, a common method of preparing the public for an announcement of a tragedy in the USSR.[](#cite_note-GalleryTimeline-73)

Around the same time, [ABC News](/wiki/ABC_News_\(United_States\) "ABC News \(United States\)") released its report about the disaster.[](#cite_note-75) Shevchenko was the first of the Ukrainian state top officials to arrive at the disaster site early on 28 April. She returned home near midnight, stopping at a radiological checkpoint in Vilcha, one of the first that were set up soon after the accident.[](#cite_note-shevchenko-64)

There was a notification from Moscow that there was no reason to postpone the 1 May [International Workers' Day](/wiki/International_Workers%27_Day "International Workers' Day") celebrations in Kiev. On 30 April a meeting of the Political bureau of the Central Committee of the CPSU took place to discuss the plan for the celebration. Scientists were reporting that the radiological background level in Kiev was normal. It was decided to shorten celebrations from the regular three and a half to four hours to under two hours.[](#cite_note-shevchenko-64)

Several buildings in Pripyat were kept open to be used by workers still involved with the plant. These included the [Jupiter factory](/wiki/Jupiter_\(Factory\) "Jupiter \(Factory\)") and the [Azure Swimming Pool](/wiki/Azure_Swimming_Pool "Azure Swimming Pool"), used by the [Chernobyl liquidators](/wiki/Chernobyl_liquidators "Chernobyl liquidators") for recreation during the clean-up.[](#cite_note-76)

### Core meltdown risk mitigation

[](/wiki/File:Chernobyl_lava_flow.jpg)Chernobyl lava-like [corium](/wiki/Corium_\(nuclear_reactor\) "Corium \(nuclear reactor\)"), formed by fuel-containing mass, flowed into the steam suppression region of the plant.[](#cite_note-Lava1-77)

#### Bubbler pools

Two floors of bubbler pools beneath the reactor served as a large water reservoir for the emergency cooling pumps and as a pressure suppression system capable of condensing steam in case of a small broken steam pipe; the third floor above them, below the reactor, served as a steam tunnel. The steam released by a broken pipe was supposed to enter the steam tunnel and be led into the pools to bubble through a layer of water.[](#cite_note-:12-24)

The smoldering steel, fuel, [serpentinite](/wiki/Serpentinite "Serpentinite") and other material, at more than 1,200 °C (2,190°F),[](#cite_note-lava2-78) started to pool on the floor of the sub-reactor space, creating [corium](/wiki/Corium_\(nuclear_reactor\) "Corium \(nuclear reactor\)"), a radioactive semi-liquid material comparable to [lava](/wiki/Lava "Lava").[](#cite_note-Lava1-77)[](#cite_note-lava3-79)[](#cite_note-80) It was feared that if this mixture travelled through the pipes into the pool of water, the resulting steam production would further contaminate the area or even cause another explosion, and that it would therefore be necessary to drain the pool.[](#cite_note-81) These fears ultimately proved unfounded, since corium began dripping harmlessly into the flooded bubbler pools before the water could be removed.[](#cite_note-auto1-82) The molten fuel hit the water and cooled into a light-brown ceramic pumice, whose low density allowed it to float.[](#cite_note-auto1-82)

[](/wiki/File:Levels_of_radioactivity_in_the_lava_under_the_Chernobyl_number_four_reactor_1986.svg)Extremely high levels of radioactivity in the lava under the Chernobyl number four reactor in 1986

Unaware of this, the government commission directed that the bubbler pools be drained by opening its [sluice gates](/wiki/Sluice_gates "Sluice gates"). The valves controlling it, however, were located in a flooded corridor in a subterranean annex adjacent to the reactor building. Engineer [Oleksiy Ananenko](/wiki/Oleksiy_Ananenko "Oleksiy Ananenko") was originally chosen for the task, as he had worked on maintenance in these corridors many times before. Ananenko reported to his superior, [Boris Baranov](/wiki/Boris_Baranov "Boris Baranov"), who elected to go with him. Finally, they chose [Valeri Bespalov](/wiki/Valery_Bespalov "Valery Bespalov") to join them with a dosimeter. In waterproof clothing, equipped with only a flashlight and dosimeters, they navigated the dark, highly radioactive corridors toward the corridors where they were able to open all valves for the sluice gates.[](#cite_note-:9-83)[](#cite_note-:10-84)[](#cite_note-:11-85) Numerous media reports falsely suggested that all three men died just days later. In fact, all three survived and were awarded the [Order for Courage](/wiki/Order_for_Courage "Order for Courage") in May 2018.[](#cite_note-:9-83)[](#cite_note-:10-84)

Once the bubbler pool gates were opened, fire brigade pumps were then used to drain the basement. The operation was not completed until 8 May, after 20,000 tonnes of water were pumped out.[](#cite_note-:11-85)

At Shcherbina's instructions, helicopter units of the [Air Forces of the Kiev Military District](/wiki/17th_Air_Army "17th Air Army") were directed to seal the crippled reactor unit no. 4 with sand. _Generál-leytenánt_ ("two stars") N.P. Kryukov and _General-mayor_ ("one star") [Nikolay Antoshkin](/wiki/Nikolay_Antoshkin "Nikolay Antoshkin"), respectively the Commander and Chief of Staff of the Air Forces of the Kiev Military District, arrived at the plant in the early morning of 27 April.[](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPotterKerner19911033–1034-86)

#### Foundation protection measures

The government commission was concerned that the molten core would burn into the earth and contaminate groundwater. To reduce the likelihood of this, it was decided to freeze the earth beneath the reactor, which would also stabilize the foundations. Using oil-[well drilling](/wiki/Well_drilling "Well drilling") equipment, injection of liquid nitrogen began on 4 May. It was estimated that 25 tonnes of liquid nitrogen per day would be required to keep the soil frozen at -100 C.[](#cite_note-MedvedevZ-19): 59 This idea was quickly scrapped.[](#cite_note-87)

As an alternative, subway builders and [coal miners](/wiki/Coal_miner "Coal miner") were deployed to excavate a tunnel below the reactor that would make room for a cooling system. The final makeshift design for the cooling system incorporated a coiled formation of pipes cooled with water, with a thin top cover of thermally-conductive graphite. The graphite layer would prevent the concrete above from melting, and was to be encapsulated between two concrete layers, each 1 m thick, for stabilisation. This graphite-concrete "sandwich" would be similar in concept to later [core catchers](/wiki/Core_catcher "Core catcher") now part of many nuclear reactor designs.[](#cite_note-88)

Following a drop in air temperatures, and reports that the fuel melt had stopped, both the graphite cooling plate and the prior nitrogen-injection proposal were abandoned. It was later determined that the fuel had flowed down three floors, with a few cubic meters coming to rest at ground level. The precautionary underground channel, with its active cooling, was deemed redundant; instead, the excavation was filled with concrete to strengthen the foundation below the reactor.[](#cite_note-89)

### Site cleanup

#### Debris removal

In the months after the explosion, attention turned to removing the radioactive debris from the roof.[](#cite_note-robotsroof-90) While the worst of the debris had remained inside what was left of the reactor, an estimated 100 tonnes of debris on the roof had to be removed to enable the safe construction of the "sarcophagus"—a concrete structure that would entomb the reactor and inhibit release of radioactive dust.[](#cite_note-robotsroof-90) The initial plan was to use robots to clear the roof. Approximately 60 remote-controlled robots were involved, primarily designed for use in lunar exploration or policing work.[](#cite_note-91)

[](/wiki/File:Robots_used_during_the_Chernobyl_cleanup_\(11384369646\).jpg) STR-1 robot used in cleanup, nicknamed "Moon Walker"

Many robots failed due to the difficult terrain, combined with the effect of high radiation fields on their batteries and electronic controls.[](#cite_note-robotsroof-90) In 1987, [Valery Legasov](/wiki/Valery_Legasov "Valery Legasov"), first deputy director of the [Kurchatov Institute of Atomic Energy](/wiki/Kurchatov_Institute_of_Atomic_Energy "Kurchatov Institute of Atomic Energy") in Moscow, said: "We learned that robots are not the great remedy for everything. Where there was very high radiation, the robot ceased to be a robot—the electronics quit working."[](#cite_note-NatGeo-92)

Consequently, the most highly radioactive materials were shovelled by [Chernobyl liquidators](/wiki/Chernobyl_liquidators "Chernobyl liquidators") from the military, wearing protective gear. These soldiers could only spend a maximum of 40–90 seconds working on the rooftops of the surrounding buildings because of the extremely high radiation levels. Only 10% of the debris cleared from the roof was performed by robots; the other 90% was removed by 3,828 men who each received, on average, an estimated dose of 25 [rem](/wiki/Roentgen_equivalent_man "Roentgen equivalent man") (250 [mSv](/wiki/Sievert "Sievert")) of radiation.[](#cite_note-robotsroof-90)

#### Construction of the sarcophagus

Main article: [Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant sarcophagus](/wiki/Chernobyl_Nuclear_Power_Plant_sarcophagus "Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant sarcophagus")

[](/wiki/File:Chernobylreactor_1.jpg)No.‍4 reactor site in 2006 showing the [sarcophagus containment structure](/wiki/Chernobyl_Nuclear_Power_Plant_sarcophagus "Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant sarcophagus"); reactor no.‍3 is to the left of the smoke stack

With the open-air reactor fire extinguished, the next step was to prevent the spread of contamination due to wind or birds. In addition, rainwater could wash contamination into the sub-surface water table, where it could migrate outside the site. Rainwater falling on the wreckage could also accelerate corrosion of steelwork in the remaining reactor structure. A further challenge was to reduce the large amount of emitted [gamma radiation](/wiki/Gamma_ray "Gamma ray"), which was a hazard to the workforce operating adjacent reactor no. 3.[](#cite_note-93)

The chosen solution was to enclose the wrecked reactor within a huge composite steel and concrete shelter, which became known as the "Sarcophagus". It had to be constructed quickly, and within the constraint of high-level ambient gamma radiation. The design started on 20 May 1986, 24 days after the disaster; construction was from June to late November.[](#cite_note-94)

The construction workers had to be protected from radiation; techniques such as crane drivers working from lead-lined control cabins were employed. The construction work included erecting walls around the perimeter, clearing and surface-concreting the surrounding ground to remove sources of radiation and to allow access for large construction machinery, constructing a thick radiation-shielding wall to protect the workers in reactor no. 3, fabricating a high-rise buttress to strengthen parts of the old structure, constructing an overall roof, and provisioning a [ventilation](/wiki/Ventilation_\(architecture\) "Ventilation \(architecture\)")-extraction system to capture airborne contamination within the shelter.[](#cite_note-ChernobylLegacy2006-95)[_[failed verification](/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_]

#### Investigations of the reactor condition

From May 5, 1986, workers from [NIKIET](https://ru.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D0%9D%D0%98%D0%9A%D0%98%D0%AD%D0%A2_%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B5%D0%BD%D0%B8_%D0%9D._%D0%90._%D0%94%D0%BE%D0%BB%D0%BB%D0%B5%D0%B6%D0%B0%D0%BB%D1%8F "ru:НИКИЭТ имени Н. А. Доллежаля") and the [V. G. Khlopin Radium Institute](/wiki/V._G._Khlopin_Radium_Institute "V. G. Khlopin Radium Institute") collaborated under the name Field Integrated Scientific and Technical Brigade No. 9 (PKNTB-9) to work at the Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant, led by G. S. Sinitsyna and S. S. Kovalenko. Their job was to conduct dosimetric surveys of both Unit 3, Unit 4, The Vent Block, and the Deaerator block, as well as assessing the damage and finding nuclear fuel.[](#cite_note-96)[](#cite_note-97)

They ultimately did not get very far, however. They were only able to provide dosimetric measurements down to the floor on +12.5, where they were unable to go any further down due to extreme radiation levels. These radiation levels were later found to be caused by [corium](/wiki/Corium_\(nuclear_reactor\) "Corium \(nuclear reactor\)") on the +9.0 level, as part of the horizontal flow.[](#cite_note-98) Their efforts would also be hampered by an electrical fire on May 23.[](#cite_note-99)

Following several models by Oleksandr Borovoi and [Valery Legasov](/wiki/Valery_Legasov "Valery Legasov"), they determined that 90% of the nuclear fuel remained inside the building. Now, needing to locate where exactly, members of the [Kurchatov Institute](/wiki/Kurchatov_Institute "Kurchatov Institute") came to Chernobyl on June 6, being led by Konstantin Checherov, began attempting to find routes into these high radiation areas. As previously assessed, they could not get to these lower levels from +12.5 due to extreme radiation levels coming from the staircase 257. On June 10, Konstantin Checherov measured a radiation level of 11,400 roentgens per hour inside an open hatch within the Southern Main Circulation Pump Hall. This radiation would later be assessed to also be caused by Corium.[](#cite_note-100)

Around June 15, Mikhail Kostyakov and Vladimir Kabanov were attempting to access the lower levels from the southern side. From corridor 017/2 on +0.0, they began to ascend a staircase leading to corridor 217/2 on +6.0. However, during this ascent, their dosimeter failed and broke, so they turned back. They did not know it but they had just been the first people to come across the [Elephant's Foot.](/wiki/Elephant%27s_Foot_\(Chernobyl\) "Elephant's Foot \(Chernobyl\)")[](#cite_note-101)[](#cite_note-102)

Following the start of the construction of the Sarcophagus, all expedition work had to be stopped, as concrete was being actively poured into the buildings. It would not be until after the Sarcophagus was complete - in November - that work could begin.[](#cite_note-103)

In this time, Konstantin Checherov alongside several figures such as Oleksandr Borovoy formed the "Complex Expedition". Their job was to assess the condition of the building and also locate the fuel and determine if it could go critical again. It was a major concern among the Complex Expedition regarding the composition of the molten fuel, as if the molten fuel contained enough fuel and a moderator, in theory it could create more damage to the building, or even another explosion.[](#cite_note-104)

The work began once again in late November 1986, as the Complex Expedition began work to assess the damage, while members of the Khoplin Radium Institute collected any fuel rods they found and took them back to their laboratory.[](#cite_note-105)

In December 1986, after months of investigation, Vasya Koryagin accidentally found a large object that appeared to be molten lead that had dropped into the reactor core and melted. The mass was called "[The Elephant's Foot](/wiki/Elephant%27s_Foot_\(Chernobyl\) "Elephant's Foot \(Chernobyl\)")" for its wrinkled appearance.[](#cite_note-106) Only following samples being obtained were they able to assess that it was composed of melted sand, concrete, and a large amount of nuclear fuel that had escaped from the reactor. The concrete beneath the reactor was steaming hot, and was breached by now-solidified lava and spectacular unknown crystalline forms termed [chernobylite](/wiki/Chernobylite "Chernobylite"). It was concluded that there was no further risk of explosion.[](#cite_note-BBCContaining-56)

### Area cleanup

[](/wiki/File:M%C3%A9dailles_liquidateurs.jpg)Soviet badge and medal awarded to [Chernobyl liquidators](/wiki/Chernobyl_liquidators "Chernobyl liquidators") [](/wiki/File:20110426-IWHO-22.jpg)Portraits of deceased [Chernobyl liquidators](/wiki/Chernobyl_liquidators "Chernobyl liquidators") used for an [anti-nuclear](/wiki/Anti-nuclear "Anti-nuclear") power protest in [Geneva](/wiki/Geneva "Geneva")

The official contaminated zones saw a massive clean-up effort lasting seven months.[](#cite_note-MarplesSocialImpact-66): 177–183 The official reason for such early, and dangerous, decontamination efforts, rather than allowing time for natural decay, was that the land needed to be repopulated and brought back into cultivation. Within fifteen months, 75% of the land was under cultivation, even though only a third of the evacuated villages were resettled. Yet this land was of marginal agricultural value. According to David Marples, the administration wished to forestall panic regarding nuclear energy, and even to restart the power station.[](#cite_note-MarplesSocialImpact-66): 78–79, 87, 192–193

Helicopters regularly sprayed large areas of contaminated land with "Barda", a sticky polymerizing fluid, designed to entrap radioactive dust.[](#cite_note-107) Although a number of radioactive emergency vehicles were buried in trenches, many of the vehicles used by the liquidators still remained, as of 2018, parked in a field in the Chernobyl area. Scavengers have removed many functioning, but highly radioactive, parts.[](#cite_note-108)

A unique "clean up" medal was given to the clean-up workers, known as "liquidators".[](#cite_note-109) Liquidators worked under poor conditions, uninformed and with little protection. Many of them exceeded radiation safety limits.[](#cite_note-MarplesSocialImpact-66): 177–183 [](#cite_note-PetrynaLE-110)

## Site remediation

Questions arose about the future of the plant and its fate. All work on the unfinished reactors no. 5 and no. 6 was halted three years later.[](#cite_note-111) The damaged reactor was sealed off and 200 cubic metres of concrete was placed between the disaster site and the operational buildings. The [Ukrainian government](/wiki/Government_of_Ukraine "Government of Ukraine") allowed the three remaining reactors to continue operating because of an energy shortage.[](#cite_note-112)

In October 1991, a fire occurred in the turbine building of reactor no. 2;[](#cite_note-113) the authorities subsequently declared the reactor damaged beyond repair, and it was taken offline. Reactor no. 1 was decommissioned in November 1996 as part of a deal between the Ukrainian government and international organizations such as the IAEA to end operations at the plant. On 15 December 2000, then-President [Leonid Kuchma](/wiki/Leonid_Kuchma "Leonid Kuchma") personally turned off reactor no. 3 in an official ceremony, shutting down the entire site.[](#cite_note-114)

### No. 4 reactor confinement

Further information: [Chernobyl New Safe Confinement](/wiki/Chernobyl_New_Safe_Confinement "Chernobyl New Safe Confinement")

[](/wiki/File:NSC-Oct-2017.jpg)[Chernobyl New Safe Confinement](/wiki/Chernobyl_New_Safe_Confinement "Chernobyl New Safe Confinement") in 2017

The concrete sarcophagus which had been built in the months after the accident was never intended to last very long, with an expected lifespan of only 30 years. On 12 February 2013, a 600 square metre section of the roof of the turbine-building collapsed, adjacent to the sarcophagus, causing a new release of radioactivity. At first, it was assumed that this was due to the weight of snow; however, the quantity of snow was not exceptional, and the report of a Ukrainian fact-finding panel concluded that the collapse was the result of sloppy repair work and aging of the structure. Experts warned the sarcophagus itself was on the verge of collapse.[](#cite_note-115)[](#cite_note-116)

In 1997, the international [Chernobyl Shelter Fund](/wiki/Chernobyl_Shelter_Fund "Chernobyl Shelter Fund") was founded to design and build a more permanent cover for the unstable sarcophagus. It received €864 million from international donors in 2011 and was managed by the [European Bank for Reconstruction and Development](/wiki/European_Bank_for_Reconstruction_and_Development "European Bank for Reconstruction and Development").[](#cite_note-117) The new shelter was named the [New Safe Confinement](/wiki/New_Safe_Confinement "New Safe Confinement") (NSC), and construction began in 2010. It consisted of a metal arch 105 m (344 ft) high and spanning 257 m (843 ft), built on rails adjacent to the reactor no. 4 building so that it could be slid over the top of the existing sarcophagus. The shelter was completed in 2016 and was moved into place over the sarcophagus on 29 November.[](#cite_note-118)

In February 2025, a Russian drone struck the shelter causing a fire, damaging the outer and inner protective covering of the NSC, and insulating layers inside.[](#cite_note-119)[](#cite_note-120)[](#cite_note-121) In December 2025 the IAEA said that the drone strike meant that the structure could no longer perform its main safety function.[](#cite_note-:8-122) IAEA Director General [Rafael Grossi](/wiki/Rafael_Grossi "Rafael Grossi") stated that an IAEA mission in late November "confirmed that the [protective structure] had lost its primary safety functions, including the confinement capability, but also found that there was no permanent damage to its load-bearing structures or monitoring systems".[](#cite_note-:8-122)

### Waste management

Used fuel from units 1–3 was stored in the units' cooling ponds, and in an interim spent fuel storage facility pond, ISF-1, which now holds most of the spent fuel from units 1–3, allowing those reactors to be decommissioned under less restrictive conditions. Approximately 50 of the fuel assemblies from units 1 and 2 were damaged and required special handling. Moving fuel to ISF-1 was carried out in three stages: fuel from unit 3 was moved first, then all undamaged fuel from units 1 and 2, and finally the damaged fuel from units 1 and 2. Fuel transfers to ISF-1 were completed in June 2016.[](#cite_note-123)

A need was recognised for larger, longer-term [radioactive waste](/wiki/Radioactive_waste "Radioactive waste") management at the site, and a new facility designated ISF-2 was designed. This serves as dry storage for used fuel assemblies from units 1–3 and other operational wastes, as well as material from decommissioning units 1–3. A contract was signed in 1999 with Areva NP ([Framatome](/wiki/Framatome "Framatome")) for construction of ISF-2. In 2003, after a significant part of the storage structures had been built, technical deficiencies in the design concept became apparent. In 2007, Areva withdrew and [Holtec International](/wiki/Holtec_International "Holtec International") was contracted for a new design and construction of ISF-2. The new design was approved in 2010, work started in 2011, and construction was completed in August 2017.[](#cite_note-124)

ISF-2 is the world's largest nuclear fuel storage facility, expected to hold more than 21,000 fuel assemblies for at least 100 years. The project includes a processing facility able to cut the fuel assemblies and to place the material in canisters, to be filled with [inert gas](/wiki/Inert_gas "Inert gas") and welded shut. The canisters are then to be transported to [dry storage vaults](/wiki/Dry_cask_storage "Dry cask storage"), where the fuel containers will be enclosed for up to 100 years. Expected processing capacity is 2,500 fuel assemblies per year.[](#cite_note-WNA-Chernobyl-125)

#### Fuel-containing materials

The radioactive material consists of core fragments, dust, and lava-like "fuel containing materials" (FCM)—also called " [corium](/wiki/Corium_\(nuclear_reactor\) "Corium \(nuclear reactor\)")"[](#cite_note-Kitagaki2023-126)—that flowed through the wrecked reactor building before hardening into a [ceramic](/wiki/Ceramic "Ceramic") form.[](#cite_note-Lava1-77)

Three different lavas are present in the basement of the reactor building: black, brown, and a [porous](/wiki/Porous "Porous") ceramic. The lava materials are [silicate glasses](/wiki/Silicate_glass "Silicate glass") with [inclusions](/wiki/Inclusion_\(mineral\) "Inclusion \(mineral\)") of other materials within them. The porous lava is brown lava that dropped into water and thus cooled rapidly. It is unclear how long the ceramic form will retard the release of radioactivity. From 1997 to 2002, a series of published papers suggested that the self-irradiation of the lava would convert all 1200 tonnes into a submicrometre and mobile powder within a few weeks.[](#cite_note-127)

According to a published paper, degradation of the lava is likely to be a slow, gradual process.[](#cite_note-Borovoi2006-128) The same paper states that the loss of [uranium](/wiki/Uranium "Uranium") from the wrecked reactor is only 10 kg per year; this low rate of uranium leaching suggests that the lava is resisting its environment.[](#cite_note-Borovoi2006-128) The paper also states that when the shelter is improved, the leaching rate of the lava will decrease.[](#cite_note-Borovoi2006-128) As of 2021, some fuel had already degraded significantly. The 'elephant's foot', which originally was so hard that it required the use of an armour piercing [AK-47](/wiki/AK-47 "AK-47") round to remove a chunk, had softened to a texture similar to sand.[](#cite_note-:3-129)[](#cite_note-Higginbotham-130)

Prior to the completion of the New Safe Confinement building, rainwater acted as a [neutron moderator](/wiki/Neutron_moderator "Neutron moderator"), triggering increased fission in the remaining materials, risking criticality. [Gadolinium nitrate](/wiki/Gadolinium_nitrate "Gadolinium nitrate") solution was used to quench neutrons to slow the fission. Even after completion of the building, fission reactions may have increased. While neutron activity has declined across most of the destroyed fuel, from 2017 until late 2020 a doubling in neutron density was recorded in the sub-reactor space, before levelling off in early 2021. This indicated increasing levels of fission as water levels dropped, the opposite of what had been expected, and atypical compared to other fuel-containing areas. The fluctuations have led to fears that a self-sustaining reaction could be created, which would likely spread more radioactive dust and debris throughout the New Safe Confinement, making future cleanup even more difficult. Potential solutions include using a robot to drill into the fuel and insert boron carbide control rods.[](#cite_note-:3-129) In early 2021, a ChNPP press release stated that the observed increase in neutron densities had levelled off since the beginning of that year.[](#cite_note-131)

### Exclusion zone

Further information: [Chernobyl Exclusion Zone](/wiki/Chernobyl_Exclusion_Zone "Chernobyl Exclusion Zone")

[](/wiki/File:Map_of_Chernobyl_Exclusion_Zone.svg)Map of the Exclusion Zone [](/wiki/File:Checkpoint_ditkatky_chernobyl_zone.JPG)The entrance to the [zone of alienation](/wiki/Chernobyl_Exclusion_Zone "Chernobyl Exclusion Zone") around Chernobyl

The Exclusion Zone was originally an area with a radius of 30 km in all directions from the plant, but was subsequently greatly enlarged to include an area measuring approximately 2600 square km, officially called the "[zone of alienation](/wiki/Chernobyl_Exclusion_Zone "Chernobyl Exclusion Zone")". The area has largely reverted to forest and was overrun by wildlife due to the lack of human competition for space and resources.[](#cite_note-Telegraph2016-132)

Mass media sources have provided generalized estimates for when the Zone could be considered [habitable](/wiki/Habitability "Habitability") again. These informal estimates have ranged[](#cite_note-csmonitor-133) from approximately 300 years[](#cite_note-134) to multiples of 20,000 years,[](#cite_note-csmonitor-133) referring to the half-life of Plutonium-239 which contaminates the central portion of the Zone. 

In the years following the disaster, residents known as _[samosely](/wiki/Samosely "Samosely")_ illegally returned to their abandoned homes. Most people are retired and survive mainly from farming and packages delivered by visitors.[](#cite_note-135)[](#cite_note-136) As of 2016[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Chernobyl_disaster&action=edit), 187 locals had returned to the zone and were living permanently there.[](#cite_note-Telegraph2016-132)

In 2011, Ukraine opened the sealed zone around the Chernobyl reactor to tourists.[](#cite_note-137)[](#cite_note-138)[](#cite_note-Distillations-139)[](#cite_note-Digges-140)

#### Forest fire concerns

See also: [Polesie State Radioecological Reserve](/wiki/Polesie_State_Radioecological_Reserve "Polesie State Radioecological Reserve")

During the dry season, [forest fires](/wiki/Wildfire "Wildfire") are a perennial concern in areas contaminated by radioactive material. Dry conditions and build-up of debris make the forests a ripe breeding ground for wildfires.[](#cite_note-141) Depending on prevailing atmospheric conditions, smoke from wildfires could potentially spread more radioactive material outside the exclusion zone.[](#cite_note-142)[](#cite_note-143)

In April 2020, forest fires spread through 20,000 ha of the exclusion zone, causing increased radiation from the release of caesium-137 and strontium-90 from the ground and biomass. The increase in radioactivity was detectable by the monitoring network but did not pose a threat to human health. The average radiation dose that Kyiv residents received as a result of the fires was estimated to be 1 nSv.[](#cite_note-144)[](#cite_note-145)

### Recovery projects

The Chernobyl Trust Fund was created in 1991 by the United Nations to help victims of the Chernobyl accident.[](#cite_note-146) It is administered by the [United Nations Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs](/wiki/United_Nations_Office_for_the_Coordination_of_Humanitarian_Affairs "United Nations Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs"), which also manages strategy formulation, resource mobilization, and advocacy efforts.[](#cite_note-:1-147) Beginning in 2002, under the [United Nations Development Programme](/wiki/United_Nations_Development_Programme "United Nations Development Programme"), the fund shifted its focus from emergency assistance to long-term development.[](#cite_note-:0-148)[](#cite_note-:1-147)

The [Chernobyl Shelter Fund](/wiki/Chernobyl_Shelter_Fund "Chernobyl Shelter Fund") was established in 1997 at the [G8 summit](/wiki/23rd_G8_summit "23rd G8 summit") in Denver to finance the Shelter Implementation Plan (SIP). The plan called for transforming the site into an ecologically safe condition through stabilization of the sarcophagus and construction of the [New Safe Confinement](/wiki/Chernobyl_New_Safe_Confinement "Chernobyl New Safe Confinement") structure. While the original cost estimate for the SIP was US$768 million, the 2006 estimate was $1.2 billion.[](#cite_note-GAO-07-923-149)

In 2003, the United Nations Development Programme launched the [Chernobyl Recovery and Development Programme](/wiki/Chernobyl_Recovery_and_Development_Programme "Chernobyl Recovery and Development Programme") (CRDP) for the recovery of affected areas.[](#cite_note-150) The programme was initiated in February 2002 based on the recommendations in the report on Human Consequences of the Chernobyl Nuclear Accident. The main goal of the CRDP was to support the [Government of Ukraine](/wiki/Government_of_Ukraine "Government of Ukraine") in mitigating the long-term social, economic, and ecological consequences of the Chernobyl catastrophe. CRDP works in the four most affected Ukrainian areas: [Kyivska](/wiki/Kyivska "Kyivska"), [Zhytomyrska](/wiki/Zhytomyr_Oblast "Zhytomyr Oblast"), [Chernihivska](/wiki/Chernihiv_Oblast "Chernihiv Oblast") and [Rivnenska](/wiki/Rivnenska "Rivnenska").[](#cite_note-151)[_[failed verification](/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_]

More than 18,000 Ukrainian children affected by the disaster have been treated in the [resort town](/wiki/Resort_town "Resort town") of [Tarará](/wiki/Tarar%C3%A1 "Tarará"), Cuba, since 1990.[](#cite_note-152)

The International Programme on the Health Effects of the Chernobyl Accident was created and received US$20 million, mainly from Japan, in the hope of discovering the main cause of health problems due to [iodine-131](/wiki/Iodine-131 "Iodine-131") radiation.[](#cite_note-153)[_[failed verification](/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_] These funds were divided among Ukraine, Belarus, and Russia for the investigation of health effects.[](#cite_note-154)

### Tourism

The first limited guided tours began in 2002.[](#cite_note-155) The 2007 release of the video game _[S.T.A.L.K.E.R.](/wiki/S.T.A.L.K.E.R. "S.T.A.L.K.E.R.")_ increased the site's popularity,[](#cite_note-mettler-156) and tour operators estimated that 40,000 tourists visited the site between 2007 and 2017.[](#cite_note-157) Between 2017 and 2022, over 350,000 tourists visited the site, hitting the maximum peak of almost 125,000 visitors in 2019, coinciding with the release of HBO's mini-series about the disaster.[](#cite_note-158)[](#cite_note-159) After its release in July 2019, Ukrainian president [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](/wiki/Volodymyr_Zelenskyy "Volodymyr Zelenskyy") announced that the Chernobyl site would become an official tourist attraction. Zelenskyy said, "We must give this territory of Ukraine a new life."[](#cite_note-160)[](#cite_note-161) Dr. T.
