Steen, a [microbiology](/wiki/Microbiology "Microbiology") and [immunology](/wiki/Immunology "Immunology") teacher at Georgetown's School of Medicine, recommends that tourists wear clothes and shoes they are comfortable throwing away and to avoid plant life.[](#cite_note-mettler-156) Tourism rebounded after COVID in 2021, but the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](/wiki/2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine "2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine") in early 2022 and fighting in the surrounding area forced it to be closed to all visitors.[](#cite_note-162) The zone remains closed to tourists as of April 2026.[](#cite_note-163)

A parallel "stalker" subculture developed of illegal visitors roaming the area for prolonged periods,[](#cite_note-164) with some hiking into the zone over 100 times.[](#cite_note-165) These visitors are noted as often failing to take appropriate precautions against radiation.[](#cite_note-166)

## Long-term effects

Main article: [Effects of the Chernobyl disaster](/wiki/Effects_of_the_Chernobyl_disaster "Effects of the Chernobyl disaster")

### Release and spread of radioactive materials

Although it is difficult to compare the Chernobyl accident with a deliberate [air burst](/wiki/Air_burst "Air burst") nuclear detonation, it is estimated that Chernobyl released about 400 times more radioactive material than the [atomic bombings of Hiroshima and Nagasaki](/wiki/Atomic_bombings_of_Hiroshima_and_Nagasaki "Atomic bombings of Hiroshima and Nagasaki") combined. Despite this, the Chernobyl disaster released only about one-hundredth to one-thousandth of the total radioactivity released during [nuclear weapons testing](/wiki/Nuclear_weapons_testing "Nuclear weapons testing") at the height of the [Cold War](/wiki/Cold_War "Cold War"), due to varying isotope abundances.[](#cite_note-167)

Approximately 100,000 square km of land was significantly contaminated, with the worst-affected areas in Belarus, Ukraine, and [Russia](/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic "Russian Soviet Federative Socialist Republic").[](#cite_note-MarplesDecade-168) Lower contamination levels were detected across Europe, except for the [Iberian Peninsula](/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula "Iberian Peninsula").[](#cite_note-torch-169)[](#cite_note-RFI_24-170) On 28 April, workers at the Forsmark Nuclear Power Plant, 1100 km from Chernobyl, were found with radioactive particles on their clothing.[](#cite_note-171) Sweden's elevated radioactivity levels, detected at noon on 28 April, were traced back to the western Soviet Union.[](#cite_note-172) Meanwhile, in Finland, the [Finnish Meteorological Institute](/wiki/Finnish_Meteorological_Institute "Finnish Meteorological Institute")'s measuring instruments located in [Nurmijärvi](/wiki/Nurmij%C3%A4rvi "Nurmijärvi") detected rising radiation levels on 27 April, but a civil service strike delayed the response and publication.[](#cite_note-173)[](#cite_note-174)

Contamination from the Chernobyl accident was scattered irregularly depending on weather conditions, much of it deposited on mountainous regions such as the [Alps](/wiki/Alps "Alps"), the [Welsh](/wiki/Wales "Wales") mountains and the [Scottish Highlands](/wiki/Scottish_Highlands "Scottish Highlands"), where [adiabatic cooling](/wiki/Adiabatic_process "Adiabatic process") caused radioactive rainfall. The resulting patches of contamination were often highly localized, and localized water-flows contributed to large variations in radioactivity over small areas. Sweden and [Norway](/wiki/Norway "Norway") also received heavy fallout when the contaminated air collided with a cold front, bringing rain.[](#cite_note-GouldFire-175): 43–44, 78 There was also [groundwater contamination](/wiki/Chernobyl_groundwater_contamination "Chernobyl groundwater contamination"). 

Rain was deliberately [seeded](/wiki/Cloud_seeding "Cloud seeding") over 10,000 square km of Belarus by the [Soviet Air Force](/wiki/Soviet_Air_Force "Soviet Air Force") to remove radioactive particles from clouds heading toward highly populated areas. Heavy, black-coloured rain fell on the city of [Gomel](/wiki/Gomel "Gomel").[](#cite_note-176) Reports from Soviet and Western scientists indicate that the Belarusian SSR received about 60% of the contamination that fell on the former Soviet Union. However, the 2006 TORCH report stated that up to half of the volatile particles had actually landed outside the former USSR area currently making up Ukraine, Belarus, and Russia. An unconnected large area in [Russian SFSR](/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic "Russian Soviet Federative Socialist Republic") south of [Bryansk](/wiki/Bryansk "Bryansk") was also contaminated, as were parts of northwestern [Ukrainian SSR](/wiki/Ukrainian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic "Ukrainian Soviet Socialist Republic"). Studies in surrounding countries indicate that more than one million people could have been affected by radiation.[](#cite_note-WNA-Chernobyl-125) 2016 data from a long-term monitoring program[](#cite_note-177) showed a decrease in internal [radiation exposure](/wiki/Ionizing_radiation "Ionizing radiation") of the inhabitants of a region in Belarus close to Gomel. 

#### Relative isotopic abundances

Main article: [Behavior of nuclear fuel during a reactor accident § Chernobyl release](/wiki/Behavior_of_nuclear_fuel_during_a_reactor_accident#Chernobyl_release "Behavior of nuclear fuel during a reactor accident")

The Chernobyl release was characterized by the physical and chemical properties of the radio-isotopes in the core. Particularly dangerous were the highly radioactive [fission products](/wiki/Fission_products "Fission products"), those with high [nuclear decay](/wiki/Nuclear_decay "Nuclear decay") rates that accumulate in the food chain, such as some of the isotopes of [iodine](/wiki/Iodine "Iodine"), [caesium](/wiki/Caesium "Caesium") and [strontium](/wiki/Strontium "Strontium"). Iodine-131 was and caesium-137 remains the two most responsible for the radiation exposure received by the general population.[](#cite_note-OECD02-Ch2-2)

[](/wiki/File:AirDoseChernobylVector.svg)Contributions of the various isotopes to the atmospheric [absorbed dose](/wiki/Absorbed_dose "Absorbed dose") in the contaminated area of Pripyat, from soon after the accident to 27 years after the accident [](/wiki/File:Totalexternaldoseratecher.png)[Logarithmic scaled](/wiki/Logarithmic_scale "Logarithmic scale") graph of the [external relative gamma dose](/wiki/Equivalent_dose "Equivalent dose") for a person in the open near the disaster site. The dose that was calculated is the [relative](/wiki/Equivalent_dose "Equivalent dose") external gamma dose rate for a person standing in the open. The exact dose to a person in the real world requires a personnel-specific [radiation dose reconstruction](/wiki/Radiation_dose_reconstruction "Radiation dose reconstruction") analysis and whole body count exams.[](#cite_note-nih.gov-178)

At different times after the accident, different [isotopes](/wiki/Isotope "Isotope") were responsible for the majority of the external dose. The remaining quantity of any radioisotope, and therefore the activity of that isotope, after 7 decay [half-lives](/wiki/Half-life "Half-life") have passed, is less than 1% of its initial magnitude,[](#cite_note-179) and it continues to reduce beyond 0.78% after 7 half-lives to 0.10% remaining after 10 half-lives have passed and so on.[](#cite_note-180)[](#cite_note-181) Some radionuclides have decay products that are likewise radioactive, which is not accounted for here. The release of radioisotopes from the nuclear fuel was largely controlled by their [boiling points](/wiki/Boiling_point "Boiling point"), and the majority of the [radioactivity](/wiki/Radioactivity "Radioactivity") present in the core was retained in the reactor. 

  * All of the [noble gases](/wiki/Noble_gas "Noble gas"), including [krypton](/wiki/Krypton "Krypton") and [xenon](/wiki/Xenon "Xenon"), contained within the reactor were released immediately into the atmosphere by the first steam explosion.[](#cite_note-OECD02-Ch2-2) The atmospheric release of [xenon-133](/wiki/Xenon-133 "Xenon-133"), with a half-life of 5 days, is estimated at 5200 PBq.[](#cite_note-OECD02-Ch2-2)
  * 50 to 60% of all core [radioiodine](/wiki/Radioiodine "Radioiodine") in the reactor, about 1760 [PBq](/wiki/Becquerel "Becquerel") (1760×1015 becquerels), or about 400 g, was released, as a mixture of [sublimed](/wiki/Sublimation_\(phase_transition\) "Sublimation \(phase transition\)") [vapour](/wiki/Vapor "Vapor"), solid particles, and [organic iodine](/wiki/Organoiodine_compound "Organoiodine compound") [compounds](/wiki/Chemical_compound "Chemical compound"). Iodine-131 has a half-life of 8 days.[](#cite_note-OECD02-Ch2-2)
  * 20 to 40% of all core [caesium-137](/wiki/Caesium-137 "Caesium-137") was released, 85 PBq in all.[](#cite_note-OECD02-Ch2-2)[](#cite_note-182) Caesium was released in [aerosol](/wiki/Particulate "Particulate") form; caesium-137, along with [isotopes of strontium](/wiki/Isotopes_of_strontium "Isotopes of strontium"), are the two primary elements preventing the Chernobyl exclusion zone being re-inhabited.[](#cite_note-stanford1-183) 8.5×1016 Bq equals 24 kilograms of caesium-137.[](#cite_note-stanford1-183) Cs-137 has a half-life of 30 years.[](#cite_note-OECD02-Ch2-2)
  * [Tellurium-132](/wiki/Tellurium-132 "Tellurium-132"), half-life 78 hours, an estimated 1150 PBq was released.[](#cite_note-OECD02-Ch2-2)
  * An early estimate for total [nuclear fuel](/wiki/Nuclear_fuel "Nuclear fuel") material released to the environment was 3±1.5%; this was later revised to 3.5±0.5%. This corresponds to the atmospheric emission of 6 tonnes of fragmented fuel.[](#cite_note-OECD1995-184)

### Environmental impact

Main article: [Effects of the Chernobyl disaster § Long-term effects on plant and animal health](/wiki/Effects_of_the_Chernobyl_disaster#Long-term_effects_on_plant_and_animal_health "Effects of the Chernobyl disaster")

#### Water bodies

The Chernobyl nuclear power plant is located next to the Pripyat River, which feeds into the Dnieper reservoir system, one of the largest surface water systems in Europe, which at the time supplied water to Kiev's 2.4 million residents, and was still in spring flood when the accident occurred.[](#cite_note-MarplesSocialImpact-66): 60 The radioactive contamination of aquatic systems therefore became a major problem in the immediate aftermath.[](#cite_note-smithber05-185)

In the most affected areas of Ukraine, levels of radioactivity in drinking water caused concern during the weeks and months after the accident.[](#cite_note-smithber05-185) Guidelines for levels of radioiodine in drinking water were temporarily raised to 3,700 [Bq](/wiki/Becquerel "Becquerel")/L, allowing most water to be reported as safe.[](#cite_note-smithber05-185) Officially it was stated that all contaminants had settled to the bottom "in an insoluble phase" and would not dissolve for 800–1000 years.[](#cite_note-MarplesSocialImpact-66): 64

[Groundwater](/wiki/Groundwater "Groundwater") was not badly affected by the Chernobyl accident since [radionuclides](/wiki/Radionuclide "Radionuclide") with short half-lives decayed long before they could affect groundwater supplies, and longer-lived radionuclides such as radiocaesium and radiostrontium were [adsorbed](/wiki/Adsorption "Adsorption") to surface [soils](/wiki/Soil "Soil") before they could transfer to groundwater.[](#cite_note-IAEA-186) However, significant transfers of radionuclides to groundwater have occurred from [waste disposal](/wiki/Waste_disposal "Waste disposal") sites in the 30 km exclusion zone around Chernobyl. Although there is a potential for transfer of radionuclides from these disposal sites off-site, the IAEA Chernobyl Report argues that this is not significant in comparison to [washout](/wiki/Washout_\(erosion\) "Washout \(erosion\)") of surface-deposited radioactivity.[](#cite_note-IAEA-186)

#### Flora and fauna

[](/wiki/File:Kiev-UkrainianNationalChernobylMuseum_15.jpg)Piglet with [dipygus](/wiki/Dipygus "Dipygus") on exhibit at the [Ukrainian National Chernobyl Museum](/wiki/Ukrainian_National_Chernobyl_Museum "Ukrainian National Chernobyl Museum")

After the disaster, 4 square km (2.48 square miles) of [pine forest](/wiki/Pine "Pine") directly downwind of the reactor turned reddish-brown and died, earning the name "[Red Forest](/wiki/Red_Forest "Red Forest")".[](#cite_note-bbcmulvey-187) Some animals in the worst-hit areas also died or stopped reproducing. Most [domestic animals](/wiki/Domestic_animal "Domestic animal") were removed from the exclusion zone, but horses left on an island in the Pripyat River 6 km from the power plant died when their [thyroid](/wiki/Thyroid "Thyroid") glands were destroyed by radiation doses of 150–200 Sv.[](#cite_note-iaea1991-188) Some cattle on the same island died and those that survived were stunted. The next generation appeared to be normal.[](#cite_note-iaea1991-188) The mutation rates for plants and animals have increased by a factor of 20 because of the release of radionuclides from Chernobyl. There is evidence for elevated mortality rates and increased rates of reproductive failure in contaminated areas, consistent with the expected frequency of deaths due to mutations.[](#cite_note-189)

On farms in [Narodychi Raion](/wiki/Narodychi_Raion "Narodychi Raion") of Ukraine it is claimed that from 1986 to 1990 nearly 350 animals were born with gross deformities; in comparison, only three abnormal births had been registered in the five years prior.[](#cite_note-190)

Subsequent research on microorganisms, while limited, suggests that in the aftermath of the disaster, bacterial and viral specimens exposed to the radiation underwent rapid changes.[](#cite_note-Yablokov2009-191) Activations of soil micromycetes have been reported.[](#cite_note-Yablokov2009-191) A paper in 1998 reported the discovery of an _[Escherichia coli](/wiki/Escherichia_coli "Escherichia coli")_ mutant that was hyper-resistant to a variety of DNA-damaging elements, including x-ray radiation, [UV-C](/wiki/UV-C "UV-C"), and [4-nitroquinoline 1-oxide](/wiki/4-nitroquinoline_1-oxide "4-nitroquinoline 1-oxide") (4NQO).[](#cite_note-192)

#### Human food chain

With [radiocaesium binding less with humic acid, peaty soils](/wiki/Environmental_radioactivity#Soil "Environmental radioactivity") than the known binding "fixation" that occurs on [kaolinite](/wiki/Kaolinite "Kaolinite")-rich clay soils, many marshy areas of Ukraine had the highest soil to dairy-milk transfer coefficients, of soil activity in ~ 200 kBq/m2 to dairy milk activity in Bq/L, that had ever been reported, with the transfer, from initial land activity into milk activity, ranging from 0.3−2 to 20−2 times that which was on the soil.[](#cite_note-nih.gov-178)

In 1987, Soviet medical teams conducted some 16,000 [whole-body count](/wiki/Whole-body_counting "Whole-body counting") examinations on inhabitants in otherwise comparatively lightly contaminated regions with good prospects for recovery. This was to determine the effect of banning local food and using only food imports on the internal body burden of radionuclides in inhabitants. Concurrent agricultural [countermeasures](/wiki/Countermeasure "Countermeasure") were used when cultivation did occur, to further reduce the soil to human transfer as much as possible. The expected highest body activity was in the first few years, where the unabated ingestion of local food resulted in the transfer of activity from soil to body. After the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union "Dissolution of the Soviet Union"), the now reduced scale initiative to monitor human body activity in these regions of Ukraine, recorded a small and gradual half-decade-long rise in internal committed dose before returning to the previous trend of observing lower body counts each year. This momentary rise is hypothesized to be due to the cessation of the Soviet food imports together with many villagers returning to older dairy food cultivation practices and large increases in wild berry and mushroom foraging.[](#cite_note-nih.gov-178)

In a 2007 paper, a robot sent into the no.‍4 reactor returned with samples of black, [melanin](/wiki/Melanin "Melanin")-rich [radiotrophic fungi](/wiki/Radiotrophic_fungus "Radiotrophic fungus") that grow on the reactor's walls.[](#cite_note-193)

In 2015, long-term empirical data showed no evidence of a negative influence of radiation on mammal abundance.[](#cite_note-194)

#### Precipitation on distant high ground

On high ground, such as mountain ranges, there is increased precipitation due to [adiabatic cooling](/wiki/Adiabatic_cooling "Adiabatic cooling").[](#cite_note-195) This resulted in localized concentrations of contaminants in distant areas; higher in Bq/m2 values to many lowland areas much closer to the source of the plume.[_(/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]

The Norwegian Agricultural Authority reported that in 2009, a total of 18,000 livestock in Norway required uncontaminated feed for a period before slaughter, to ensure that their meat had an activity below the government permitted value of [caesium](/wiki/Caesium "Caesium") per kilogram deemed suitable for human consumption. This contamination was due to residual radioactivity from Chernobyl in the mountain plants they grazed on in the wild during the summer. 1,914 sheep required uncontaminated feed for a time before slaughter during 2012, with these sheep located in only 18 of Norway's municipalities, a decrease from the 35 municipalities in 2011 and the 117 municipalities affected during 1986.[](#cite_note-thelocal1-196) The after-effects of Chernobyl on the mountain lamb industry in Norway were expected to be seen for a further 100 years, although the severity of the effects would decline over that period.[](#cite_note-197)

The United Kingdom restricted the movement of sheep from upland areas when radioactive [caesium-137](/wiki/Caesium-137 "Caesium-137") fell across parts of Northern Ireland, Wales, Scotland, and northern England. In the immediate aftermath of the disaster, the movement of a total of 4,225,000 sheep was restricted across a total of 9,700 farms, to prevent contaminated meat entering the human food chain.[](#cite_note-Guardian-2009-198) The number of sheep and farms affected has decreased since 1986. Northern Ireland was released from all restrictions in 2000, and by 2009, 369 farms containing around 190,000 sheep remained under the restrictions in Wales, Cumbria, and northern Scotland.[](#cite_note-Guardian-2009-198) The restrictions applying in Scotland were lifted in 2010, while those applying to Wales and Cumbria were lifted during 2012, meaning no farms in the UK remain restricted because of Chernobyl.[](#cite_note-Indy-Scot-2012-199)[](#cite_note-BBC-June-2012-200) The legislation used to control sheep movement and compensate farmers was revoked in 2012.[](#cite_note-UKFSA-01-201)

### Human impact

Main article: [Effects of the Chernobyl disaster § Long-term health effects](/wiki/Effects_of_the_Chernobyl_disaster#Long-term_health_effects "Effects of the Chernobyl disaster")

[](/wiki/File:View_of_Chernobyl_taken_from_Pripyat.JPG)[Pripyat](/wiki/Pripyat "Pripyat") lies abandoned with the Chernobyl facility visible in the distance. [](/wiki/File:G_radiation-level_scale_01.png) Radiation exposure to first responders at Chernobyl in comparison to a range of situations, from normal activities up to nuclear accident. Each step up the scale indicates a tenfold increase in radiation level.

#### Acute radiation effects and immediate aftermath

The only known causal deaths from the accident involved plant workers and firefighters. The reactor explosion killed two engineers, and 28 others died within three months from [acute radiation syndrome](/wiki/Acute_radiation_syndrome "Acute radiation syndrome") (ARS).[](#cite_note-:5-9) Some sources report a total initial fatality of 31,[](#cite_note-Hallenbeck_1994_15-202)[](#cite_note-:4-203) due to poorly substantiated reports of an individual who died during the evacuation of Pripyat from coronary thrombosis attributed to stress.[](#cite_note-204)

Most serious ARS cases were treated with the assistance of American specialist [Robert Peter Gale](/wiki/Robert_Peter_Gale "Robert Peter Gale"), who supervised bone marrow transplant procedures, although these were unsuccessful.[](#cite_note-205)[](#cite_note-206) The fatalities were largely due to wearing dusty, soaked uniforms causing [beta burns](/wiki/Beta_burns "Beta burns") over large areas of skin,[](#cite_note-medmagrad-207) and due to bacterial infections of the gastrointestinal tract. 

#### Long-term impact

In the 10 years following the accident, 14 more people who had been initially hospitalized died, mostly from causes unrelated to radiation exposure, with only two deaths resulting from [myelodysplastic syndrome](/wiki/Myelodysplastic_syndrome "Myelodysplastic syndrome").[](#cite_note-:5-9) Scientific consensus, supported by the [Chernobyl Forum](/wiki/Chernobyl_Forum "Chernobyl Forum"), suggests no statistically significant increase in solid cancer incidence among rescue workers.[](#cite_note-:6-208) However, childhood thyroid cancer increased, with about 4,000 new cases reported by 2002 in contaminated areas of Belarus, Russia, and Ukraine, largely due to high levels of [radioactive iodine](/wiki/Radioactive_iodine "Radioactive iodine"). The recovery rate is ~99%, with 15 terminal cases reported.[](#cite_note-:6-208) No increase in mutation rates was found among children of liquidators or those living in contaminated areas.[](#cite_note-pmid15725606-209)

Psychosomatic illness and post-traumatic stress, driven by widespread fear of radiological disease, have had a significant impact, often exacerbating health issues by fostering fatalistic attitudes and harmful behaviors.[](#cite_note-:7-210)[](#cite_note-:6-208)

By 2000, the number of Ukrainians claiming radiation-related "sufferer" status reached 3.5 million, or 5% of the population, many of whom were resettled from contaminated zones or former Chernobyl workers.[](#cite_note-PetrynaLE-110): 4–5 Increased medical surveillance after the accident led to higher recorded rates of benign conditions and cancers.[](#cite_note-MarplesDecade-168)

#### Effects of main harmful radionuclides

The four most harmful radionuclides spread from Chernobyl were [iodine-131](/wiki/Iodine-131 "Iodine-131"), [caesium-134](/wiki/Caesium-134 "Caesium-134"), [caesium-137](/wiki/Caesium-137 "Caesium-137") and [strontium-90](/wiki/Strontium-90 "Strontium-90"), with half-lives of 8 days, 2.07 years, 30.2 years and 28.8 years respectively.[](#cite_note-TORCH-211): 8 The iodine was initially viewed with less alarm than the other isotopes, because of its short half-life, but it is highly volatile and appears to have travelled furthest and caused the most severe health problems.[](#cite_note-MarplesDecade-168): 24 Strontium is the least volatile and of main concern in areas near Chernobyl.[](#cite_note-TORCH-211): 8

Iodine tends to become concentrated in the thyroid and milk glands, leading, among other things, to an increased incidence of thyroid cancers. The total ingested dose was largely from iodine and, unlike the other fission products, rapidly found its way from dairy farms to human ingestion.[](#cite_note-212) Similarly in dose reconstruction, for those evacuated at different times and from various towns, the inhalation dose was dominated by iodine (40%), along with airborne tellurium (20%) and oxides of rubidium (20%) both as equally secondary, appreciable contributors.[](#cite_note-213)

Long term hazards such as caesium tends to accumulate in vital organs such as the heart,[](#cite_note-KuchinskayaWeWill-214) while strontium accumulates in bones and may be a risk to bone-marrow and [lymphocytes](/wiki/Lymphocyte "Lymphocyte").[](#cite_note-TORCH-211): 8 Radiation is most damaging to cells that are actively dividing. In adult mammals cell division is slow, except in hair follicles, skin, bone marrow and the gastrointestinal tract, which is why vomiting and hair loss are common symptoms of acute radiation sickness.[](#cite_note-MycioWormwood-215): 42

#### Disputed investigation

The mutation rates among animals in the Chernobyl zone have been a topic of ongoing scientific debate, notably regarding the research conducted by Anders Moller and Timothy Mousseau.[](#cite_note-ChesserBaker2006-216)[](#cite_note-217) Their research, which suggests higher mutation rates among wildlife in the Chernobyl zone, has been met with criticism over the reproducibility of their findings and the methodologies used.[](#cite_note-218)[](#cite_note-219)

#### Withdrawn investigation

In 1996, geneticist Ronald Chesser and Robert Baker published a paper[](#cite_note-220) on the thriving [vole](/wiki/Vole "Vole") population within the exclusion zone, in which the central conclusion was essentially that "The mutation rate in these animals is hundreds and probably thousands of times greater than normal". This claim occurred after they had done a comparison of the [mitochondrial DNA](/wiki/Mitochondrial_DNA "Mitochondrial DNA") of the "Chernobyl voles" with that of a [control group](/wiki/Control_group "Control group") of voles from outside the region.[](#cite_note-221) The authors discovered they had incorrectly classified the [species](/wiki/Species "Species") of vole and were genetically comparing two different vole species. They issued a retraction in 1997.[](#cite_note-ChesserBaker2006-216)[](#cite_note-222)[](#cite_note-223)

#### Abortions

Following the accident, journalists encouraged public mistrust of medical professionals.[](#cite_note-kasperson160-224) This media-driven framing led to an increase in induced abortions across Europe out of fear of radiation. An estimated 150,000 elective abortions were performed worldwide due to [radiophobia](/wiki/Radiophobia "Radiophobia").[](#cite_note-kasperson160-224)[](#cite_note-Knudsen-225)[](#cite_note-Trichopoulos-226)[](#cite_note-pmid3585500-227)[](#cite_note-auto2-228)[](#cite_note-229) The statistical data exclude Soviet–Ukraine–Belarus abortion rates, which are unavailable. However, in Denmark, about 400 additional abortions were recorded, and in Greece, an increase of 2,500 terminations occurred despite the low radiation dose.[](#cite_note-Knudsen-225)[](#cite_note-Trichopoulos-226)

No significant evidence of changes in the prevalence of congenital anomalies linked to the accident has been found in Belarus or Ukraine. In Sweden and Finland, studies found no association between radioactivity and congenital malformations.[](#cite_note-pmid8516187-230) Larger studies, such as the EUROCAT database, assessed nearly a million births and found no impacts from Chernobyl. Researchers concluded that widespread fear about the effects on unborn fetuses was not justified.[](#cite_note-231)

The only robust evidence of negative pregnancy outcomes linked to the accident were the elective abortion effects due to anxiety.[](#cite_note-auto2-228) In very high doses, radiation can cause pregnancy anomalies, but the malformation of organs appears to be a [deterministic effect](/wiki/Deterministic_effect "Deterministic effect") with a [threshold dose](/wiki/Dose%E2%80%93response_relationship "Dose–response relationship").[](#cite_note-ecolo.org-232)

Studies on regions of Ukraine and Belarus suggest that around 50 children exposed in utero during weeks 8 to 25 of gestation may have experienced an increased rate of [intellectual disability](/wiki/Intellectual_disability "Intellectual disability") and lower verbal IQ.[](#cite_note-233) The [Chernobyl liquidators](/wiki/Chernobyl_liquidators "Chernobyl liquidators") fathered children without an increase in developmental anomalies or a significant rise in [germline mutations](/wiki/Germline_mutation "Germline mutation").[](#cite_note-pmid15725606-209) A 2021 study based on whole-genome sequencing of children of liquidators indicated no trans-generational genetic effects.[](#cite_note-234)

#### Cancer assessments

A report by the [International Atomic Energy Agency](/wiki/International_Atomic_Energy_Agency "International Atomic Energy Agency") examines the environmental consequences of the accident.[](#cite_note-IAEA-186) The [United Nations Scientific Committee on the Effects of Atomic Radiation](/wiki/United_Nations_Scientific_Committee_on_the_Effects_of_Atomic_Radiation "United Nations Scientific Committee on the Effects of Atomic Radiation") estimated a global [collective dose](/wiki/Collective_dose "Collective dose") from the accident equivalent to "21 additional days of world exposure to natural [background radiation](/wiki/Background_radiation "Background radiation")"; doses were far higher among 530,000 recovery workers, who averaged an extra 50 years of typical natural background radiation exposure.[](#cite_note-235)[](#cite_note-UNSCEAR_2008_D-236)[](#cite_note-UNSCEAR_GA-237)

Estimates of deaths resulting from the accident vary greatly due to differing methodologies and data. In 1994, thirty-one deaths were [directly attributed to the accident](/wiki/Chernobyl_disaster-related_deaths "Chernobyl disaster-related deaths"), all among reactor staff and emergency workers.[](#cite_note-Hallenbeck_1994_15-202)

[](/wiki/File:Belarus_radioactivity_and_thyroid_cancer.png)[Thyroid cancer](/wiki/Thyroid_cancer "Thyroid cancer") incidence in children and adolescents in Belarus

Adults, ages 19 to 34

Adolescents, ages 15 to 18

Children, ages up to 14

While widely regarded as having a cause-and-effect relationship, the [causality](/wiki/Causality "Causality") of Chernobyl with the increase in recorded rates of thyroid cancer is disputed.[](#cite_note-pmid22175034-238)

The [Chernobyl Forum](/wiki/Chernobyl_Forum "Chernobyl Forum") predicts an eventual death toll of up to 4,000 among those exposed to the highest radiation levels (200,000 emergency workers, 116,000 evacuees, and 270,000 residents of the most contaminated areas), including around 50 emergency workers who died shortly after the accident, 15 children who died of [thyroid cancer](/wiki/Thyroid_cancer "Thyroid cancer"), and a predicted 3,935 deaths from radiation-induced cancer and leukemia.[](#cite_note-who.int-239)

A 2006 paper in the _[International Journal of Cancer](/wiki/International_Journal_of_Cancer "International Journal of Cancer")_ estimated that Chernobyl may have caused about 1,000 cases of thyroid cancer and 4,000 cases of other cancers in Europe by 2006. By 2065, models predict 16,000 cases of thyroid cancer and 25,000 cases of other cancers due to the accident.[](#cite_note-240)

> [The risk projections suggest](/wiki/Linear_no-threshold_model "Linear no-threshold model") that by now  Chernobyl may have caused about 1000 cases of thyroid cancer and 4000 cases of other cancers in Europe, representing about 0.01% of all incident cancers since the accident. Models predict that by 2065 about 16,000 cases of thyroid cancer and 25,000 cases of other cancers may be expected due to radiation from the accident, whereas several hundred million cancer cases are expected from other causes.

Anti-nuclear groups, such as the [Union of Concerned Scientists](/wiki/Union_of_Concerned_Scientists "Union of Concerned Scientists") (UCS), have publicized estimates suggesting an eventual 50,000 excess cancer cases, resulting in 25,000 cancer deaths worldwide, excluding thyroid cancer.[](#cite_note-241) These figures are based on a linear no-threshold model, which the [International Commission on Radiological Protection](/wiki/International_Commission_on_Radiological_Protection "International Commission on Radiological Protection") (ICRP) advises against using for risk projections.[](#cite_note-242) The 2006 [TORCH report](/wiki/TORCH_report "TORCH report") estimated 30,000 to 60,000 excess cancer deaths worldwide.[](#cite_note-torch-169)

The [Chernobyl Forum](/wiki/Chernobyl_Forum "Chernobyl Forum") revealed in 2004 that thyroid cancer among children was one of the main health impacts of the Chernobyl accident, due to ingestion of contaminated dairy products and inhalation of [Iodine-131](/wiki/Iodine-131 "Iodine-131"). More than 4,000 cases of childhood thyroid cancer were reported, but there was no evidence of increased solid cancers or leukemia. The WHO's Radiation Program reported nine deaths out of the 4,000 thyroid cancer cases.[](#cite_note-ChernobylsLegacy-243) By 2005, UNSCEAR reported an excess of over 6,000 thyroid cancer cases among those exposed as children or adolescents.[](#cite_note-Chernobyl_health_effects-244)

Well-differentiated thyroid cancers are generally treatable, with a five-year survival rate of 96% and 92% after 30 years.[](#cite_note-245) By 2011, UNSCEAR reported 15 deaths from thyroid cancer.[](#cite_note-WHO2012-12) The IAEA states that there has been no increase in birth defects, solid cancers, or other abnormalities, corroborating UN assessments.[](#cite_note-ChernobylsLegacy-243) UNSCEAR noted the possibility of long-term genetic defects, citing a doubling of radiation-induced minisatellite [mutations](/wiki/Mutation "Mutation") among children born in 1994.[](#cite_note-246) However, the risk of thyroid cancer associated with the Chernobyl accident remains high according to published studies.[](#cite_note-Bogdanova_T-247)[](#cite_note-Dinets-248)

The German affiliate of the [International Physicians for the Prevention of Nuclear War](/wiki/International_Physicians_for_the_Prevention_of_Nuclear_War "International Physicians for the Prevention of Nuclear War") suggests that 10,000 people have been affected by thyroid cancer as of 2006, with 50,000 cases expected in the future.[](#cite_note-249)

#### Other disorders

Fred Mettler, a radiation expert, estimated 9,000 Chernobyl-related cancer deaths worldwide, noting that while small relative to normal cancer risks, the numbers are large in absolute terms.[](#cite_note-Mettler-250) The report highlighted the risks to mental health from exaggerated radiation fears, noting that labeling the affected population as "victims" contributed to a sense of helplessness.[](#cite_note-ChernobylsLegacy-243) Mettler also commented that 20 years later, the population remained unsure about radiation effects, leading to harmful behaviors.[](#cite_note-Mettler-250)

#### Long-term radiation deaths

The potential deaths from the Chernobyl disaster are heavily debated. The [World Health Organization](/wiki/World_Health_Organization "World Health Organization") predicted 4,000 future cancer deaths in surrounding countries,[](#cite_note-World_Health_Organization_report_ex-13) based on the [Linear no-threshold model](/wiki/Linear_no-threshold_model "Linear no-threshold model") (LNT), which assumes that even low doses of radiation increase cancer risk proportionally.[](#cite_note-251) The Union of Concerned Scientists estimated approximately 27,000 excess cancer deaths worldwide, using the same LNT model.[](#cite_note-Union_of_Concerned_Scientists-252)

A study by Greenpeace estimated 10,000–200,000 additional deaths in Belarus, Russia, and Ukraine from 1990 to 2004.[](#cite_note-greenpeace2006-253) The report was criticized for relying on non-peer-reviewed studies, while Gregory Härtl, a WHO spokesman, suggested its conclusions were ideologically motivated.[](#cite_note-254)

The publication _[Chernobyl: Consequences of the Catastrophe for People and the Environment](/wiki/Chernobyl:_Consequences_of_the_Catastrophe_for_People_and_the_Environment "Chernobyl: Consequences of the Catastrophe for People and the Environment")_ claimed 985,000 premature deaths, but was criticized for bias and using unverifiable sources.[](#cite_note-Balonov-255)

### Socio-economic impact

[](/wiki/File:Ukrainian_National_Chornobyl_Museum_resembling_the_reactor_that_suffered_the_catastrophic_failure_\(reactor_core_surrounded_by_blue\)_\(8601829126\).jpg)Exposition at [Ukrainian National Chernobyl Museum](/wiki/Ukrainian_National_Chernobyl_Museum "Ukrainian National Chernobyl Museum")

It is difficult to establish the total economic cost of the disaster. According to [Mikhail Gorbachev](/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev "Mikhail Gorbachev"), the Soviet Union spent 18 billion Rbls ($6.05 billion in today's dollars[](#cite_note-inflation-USGDP-256)) on containment and decontamination, virtually bankrupting itself.[](#cite_note-GorbachevBoC-257) In 2005, the total cost over 30 years for Belarus was estimated at US$235 billion.[](#cite_note-ChernobylsLegacy-243) Gorbachev later wrote that "the nuclear meltdown at Chernobyl...was perhaps the real cause of the collapse of the Soviet Union."[](#cite_note-258)

Ongoing costs remain significant; in their 2003–2005 report, the [Chernobyl Forum](/wiki/Chernobyl_Forum "Chernobyl Forum") stated that between five and seven percent of government spending in Ukraine is still related to Chernobyl, while in Belarus, over $13 billion was spent between 1991 and 2003.[](#cite_note-ChernobylsLegacy-243) In 2018, Ukraine spent five to seven percent of its national budget on recovery activities.[](#cite_note-:0-148) The economic loss is estimated at $235 billion in Belarus.[](#cite_note-:0-148)

A significant impact was the removal of 784,320 ha of agricultural land and 694,200 ha of forest from production. While much has been returned to use, agricultural costs have risen due to the need for special cultivation techniques.[](#cite_note-ChernobylsLegacy-243) Politically, the accident was significant for the new Soviet policy of [glasnost](/wiki/Glasnost "Glasnost"),[](#cite_note-ShlyGlasnost-259) and helped forge closer USSR–US relations at the end of the Cold War.[](#cite_note-PetrynaLE-110): 44–48 The disaster also became a key factor in the dissolution of the Soviet Union and shaped the 'new' [Eastern Europe](/wiki/Eastern_Europe "Eastern Europe").[](#cite_note-PetrynaLE-110): 20–21 Gorbachev stated that "More than anything else, (Chernobyl) opened the possibility of much greater freedom of expression, to the point that the (Soviet) system as we knew it could no longer continue."[](#cite_note-260)

## Significance

### Nuclear debate

Main articles: [Nuclear power debate](/wiki/Nuclear_power_debate "Nuclear power debate"), [nuclear power phase-out](/wiki/Nuclear_power_phase-out "Nuclear power phase-out"), and [anti-nuclear movement](/wiki/Anti-nuclear_movement "Anti-nuclear movement")

[](/wiki/File:Radiant_Mayday-Demo_after_CHERNOBYL.jpg)Anti-nuclear protest after the Chernobyl disaster on [May Day](/wiki/International_Workers%27_Day "International Workers' Day"), 1986 in [West Berlin](/wiki/West_Berlin "West Berlin")

Because of the distrust many had in the Soviet authorities, who engaged in a cover-up, a great deal of debate about the situation occurred in the [First World](/wiki/First_World "First World") during the early days of the event. Journalists mistrusted many professionals, and in turn encouraged the public to mistrust them as well.[](#cite_note-kasperson160-224)

The accident raised already heightened concerns about [fission reactors](/wiki/Fission_reactor "Fission reactor") worldwide, and while most concern was focused on those of the same unusual design, hundreds of disparate nuclear reactor proposals, including those under construction at Chernobyl, reactors numbers 5 and 6, were eventually cancelled. With ballooning costs as a result of new [Nuclear reactor safety system](/wiki/Nuclear_reactor_safety_system "Nuclear reactor safety system") standards and the legal and political costs in dealing with the increasingly hostile/anxious public opinion, there was a precipitous drop in the rate of new reactor construction after 1986.[](#cite_note-261)

[](/wiki/File:Anti-Atom-Demo_Berlin_Potsdamer_Platz_2011-03-26.jpg)Nuclear power protest in [Berlin](/wiki/Berlin "Berlin"), 2011 [](/wiki/File:Vert-Jean-Dupuy-1986.jpg)After Chernobyl, nuclear debate became a topic in galleries and exhibitions. Artwork by French-American [Jean Dupuy](/wiki/Jean_Dupuy_\(artist\) "Jean Dupuy \(artist\)") in 1986 dedicated to Chernobyl disaster.

The accident also raised concerns about the cavalier [safety culture](/wiki/Safety_culture "Safety culture") in the Soviet nuclear power industry, slowing industry growth and forcing the Soviet government to become less secretive about its operating procedures.[](#cite_note-Kagarlitsky-262)[[b]](#cite_note-263) The government cover-up of the Chernobyl disaster was a catalyst for [glasnost](/wiki/Glasnost "Glasnost"), which "paved the way for reforms leading to the Soviet collapse."[](#cite_note-264) Numerous structural and construction quality issues, as well as deviations from the original plant design, had been known to the KGB since at least 1973 and passed on to the [Central Committee](/wiki/Central_Committee_of_the_Communist_Party_of_the_Soviet_Union "Central Committee of the Communist Party of the Soviet Union"), which took no action and [classified](/wiki/Classified_information "Classified information") the information.[](#cite_note-265)

In Italy, political fallout from the Chernobyl accident was reflected in the outcome of the [1987 nuclear power referendum](/wiki/1987_Italian_nuclear_power_referendum "1987 Italian nuclear power referendum").[](#cite_note-:13-266) As a result, Italy began phasing out its nuclear power plants in 1988, a decision that was effectively [reversed in 2008](/wiki/Nuclear_power_in_Italy "Nuclear power in Italy"). A [2011 referendum](/wiki/2011_Italian_referendums#Nuclear_power "2011 Italian referendums") reiterated Italians' objections to nuclear power, thus abrogating the government's 2008 decision.[](#cite_note-:13-266)

In Germany, the Chernobyl accident led to the creation of a [federal environment ministry](/wiki/German_federal_environment_ministry "German federal environment ministry"). The German environmental minister was given authority over reactor safety as well, a responsibility the minister still holds today. The Chernobyl disaster is also credited with strengthening the [anti-nuclear movement in Germany](/wiki/Anti-nuclear_movement_in_Germany "Anti-nuclear movement in Germany"), which culminated in the decision to [end the use of nuclear power](/wiki/Nuclear_power_phase-out#Germany "Nuclear power phase-out") made by the 1998–2005 Schröder government.[](#cite_note-267) A temporary reversal of this policy ended with the [Fukushima nuclear disaster](/wiki/Fukushima_nuclear_disaster "Fukushima nuclear disaster").[](#cite_note-268)

In direct response to the Chernobyl disaster, a conference to create a [Convention on Early Notification of a Nuclear Accident](/wiki/Convention_on_Early_Notification_of_a_Nuclear_Accident "Convention on Early Notification of a Nuclear Accident") was called in 1986 by the [International Atomic Energy Agency](/wiki/International_Atomic_Energy_Agency "International Atomic Energy Agency"). The resulting treaty has bound members to provide notification of any [nuclear and radiation accidents](/wiki/Nuclear_and_radiation_accidents "Nuclear and radiation accidents") that occur that could affect other states, along with the [Convention on Assistance in the Case of a Nuclear Accident or Radiological Emergency](/wiki/Convention_on_Assistance_in_the_Case_of_a_Nuclear_Accident_or_Radiological_Emergency "Convention on Assistance in the Case of a Nuclear Accident or Radiological Emergency").[](#cite_note-269)

Chernobyl has been used as a case study in research concerning the root causes of such disasters, such as sleep deprivation[](#cite_note-270) and mismanagement.[](#cite_note-271)

The [State Archives of Ukraine](/wiki/State_Archival_Service_of_Ukraine "State Archival Service of Ukraine") hold an archival collection of around 1,000 declassified documents relating to the construction of the power station, the disaster, and its aftermath extending to the early 2000s. This archive was added by [UNESCO](/wiki/UNESCO "UNESCO") to its [Memory of the World International Register](/wiki/Memory_of_the_World_International_Register "Memory of the World International Register") in 2017, recognising it as documentary heritage of global importance.[](#cite_note-272)

### In popular culture

Main article: [Cultural impact of the Chernobyl disaster](/wiki/Cultural_impact_of_the_Chernobyl_disaster "Cultural impact of the Chernobyl disaster")

The Chernobyl tragedy has inspired many artists across the world to create works of art, animation, video games, theatre and cinema about the disaster. The HBO series _[Chernobyl](/wiki/Chernobyl_\(miniseries\) "Chernobyl \(miniseries\)")_ and the book _[Voices from Chernobyl](/wiki/Voices_from_Chernobyl "Voices from Chernobyl")_ by the Ukrainian-Belarusian writer [Svetlana Alexievich](/wiki/Svetlana_Alexievich "Svetlana Alexievich") are two well-known works.[](#cite_note-273) The Ukrainian artist Roman Gumanyuk created a series of artworks called "Pripyat Lights, or Chernobyl shadows" that includes 30 oil paintings about the Chernobyl accident, exhibited in 2012–2013.[](#cite_note-274)[](#cite_note-275)

The video game _[S.T.A.L.K.E.R.: Shadow of Chernobyl](/wiki/S.T.A.L.K.E.R.:_Shadow_of_Chernobyl "S.T.A.L.K.E.R.: Shadow of Chernobyl")_ , developed by [GSC Game World](/wiki/GSC_Game_World "GSC Game World") and released by [THQ](/wiki/THQ "THQ") in 2007, is a first-person shooter game set in the Exclusion zone.[](#cite_note-276) A prequel called _[S.T.A.L.K.E.R.: Clear Sky](/wiki/S.T.A.L.K.E.R.:_Clear_Sky "S.T.A.L.K.E.R.: Clear Sky")_ was released in 2008 following with a sequel _[S.T.A.L.K.E.R.: Call of Pripyat](/wiki/S.T.A.L.K.E.R.:_Call_of_Pripyat "S.T.A.L.K.E.R.: Call of Pripyat")_ released in 2010. A later instalment, [S.T.A.L.K.E.R. 2: Heart of Chornobyl](/wiki/S.T.A.L.K.E.R._2:_Heart_of_Chornobyl "S.T.A.L.K.E.R. 2: Heart of Chornobyl"), was released in 2024.[](#cite_note-277) Another video game inspired by the disaster includes [Chernobylite](/wiki/Chernobylite_\(video_game\) "Chernobylite \(video game\)"), which also takes place in the [exclusion zone](/wiki/Chernobyl_exclusion_zone "Chernobyl exclusion zone") and features environments created using 3D scans of real-world locations.[](#cite_note-278) Finally, the horror film _[Chernobyl Diaries](/wiki/Chernobyl_Diaries "Chernobyl Diaries")_ released in 2012 is about six tourists that hire a tour guide to take them to the abandoned city of [Pripyat](/wiki/Pripyat "Pripyat") where they discover they are not alone.[](#cite_note-279)

[Filmmakers](/wiki/Filmmaking "Filmmaking") have created documentaries that examine the aftermath of the disaster over the years. Documentaries like the [Oscar-winning](/wiki/Oscar-winning "Oscar-winning") _[Chernobyl Heart](/wiki/Chernobyl_Heart "Chernobyl Heart")_ released in 2003, explore how radiation affected people living in the area and information about the long-term side effects of radiation exposure.[](#cite_note-280) _The Babushkas of Chernobyl_ (2015) is a documentary about three old women ("babushkas") who decided to return to the exclusion zone after the disaster. In the documentary, the women show the polluted water, their food from radioactive gardens, and explain how they manage to survive in this exclusion zone despite the radioactive levels.[](#cite_note-281)[](#cite_note-282) The documentary _The Battle of Chernobyl_ (2006) shows rare original footage a day before the disaster in the city of Pripyat, then through different methods goes in depth on the chronological events that led to the explosion of reactor no.‍4 and the disaster response.[](#cite_note-283)[](#cite_note-284) The critically acclaimed 2019 historical drama television miniseries _[Chernobyl](/wiki/Chernobyl_\(miniseries\) "Chernobyl \(miniseries\)")_ revolves around the disaster and the cleanup efforts that followed.[_(/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]

## Notes

  1. **[^](#cite_ref-45)** Although most reports on the Chernobyl accident refer to a number of graphite fires, it is highly unlikely that the graphite itself burned. According to the [General Atomics](/wiki/General_Atomics "General Atomics") website:[](#cite_note-43) "It is often incorrectly assumed that the combustion behavior of graphite is similar to that of charcoal and coal. Numerous tests and calculations have shown that it is virtually impossible to burn high-purity, nuclear-grade graphites." On Chernobyl, the same source states: "Graphite played little or no role in the progression or consequences of the accident. The red glow observed during the Chernobyl accident was the expected color of luminescence for graphite at 700°C and not a large-scale graphite fire, as some have incorrectly assumed." Similarly, nuclear physicist Yevgeny Velikhov[](#cite_note-44) noted some two weeks after the accident, "Until now the possibility of a catastrophe really did exist: A great quantity of fuel and graphite of the reactor was in an [incandescent](/wiki/Incandescent "Incandescent") state." That is, all the nuclear-[decay heat](/wiki/Decay_heat "Decay heat") that was generated inside the uranium fuel (heat that would normally be extracted by back-up coolant pumps, in an undamaged reactor) was instead responsible for making the fuel itself and any graphite in contact with it, glow red-hot. This is contrary to the often-cited interpretation, which is that the graphite was red-hot chiefly because it was chemically [oxidizing](/wiki/Oxidizing "Oxidizing") with the air.
  2. **[^](#cite_ref-263)** "No one believed the first newspaper reports, which patently understated the scale of the catastrophe and often contradicted one another. The confidence of readers was re-established only after the press was allowed to examine the events in detail without the original censorship restrictions. The policy of openness ([glasnost](/wiki/Glasnost "Glasnost")) and 'uncompromising criticism' of outmoded arrangements had been proclaimed at the 27th Congress (of the [Communist Party of Soviet Union](/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Soviet_Union "Communist Party of Soviet Union")), but it was only in the tragic days following the Chernobyl disaster that glasnost began to change from an official slogan into an everyday practice. The truth about Chernobyl that eventually hit the newspapers opened the way to a more truthful examination of other social problems. More and more articles were written about drug abuse, crime, corruption and the mistakes of leaders of various ranks. A wave of 'bad news' swept over the readers in 1986–87, shaking the consciousness of society. Many were horrified to find out about the numerous calamities of which they had previously had no idea. It often seemed to people that there were many more outrages in the epoch of [perestroika](/wiki/Perestroika "Perestroika") than before although, in fact, they had simply not been informed about them previously." Kagarlitsky 1989, pp. 333–334.

"На АЭС с реакторами РБМК-1000 используется выбег главных циркуляционных насосов (ГЦН) как самозащита при внезапном исчезновении электропитания собственных нужд (СН). Пока не включится резервное питание, циркуляция может осуществляться за счет выбега. С этой целью для увеличения продолжительности выбега, на валу электродвигателя –привода ГЦН установлен маховик с достаточно большой маховой массой."
